SOME RECOLLECTIONS OF OLD WARRINGTON. SIXTY-FIVE YEARS AGO.

BY THE LATE THOMAS LETHERBROW. ALDERLEY EDGE, NOVEMBER 20, 1898.

Sitting here in an enforced idleness which has already lasted many weeks, my thoughts have re- verted to a letter which appeared in the columns of the" Manchester City News" some time ago. The 'writer bad conversed with a man who had assisted in the construction of the railway line between Man- chester and Liverpool, over sixty years ago, who, he' seemed to think, was the only survivor of that band of workers who were the pioneers of our vast rail- way system. Yet, curiously enough, the hand that traces these lines, then a very, small one, helped to shape some of its accessories. It way in my grand- father's workshop at Warrington, and while I sit musing here the whole scene Comes back tome with its pictureeque interior,and the faces and voices long since swallowed up in the grave. As for good Peter Phillips himself there is barely anyone in the old town who remembers him, though the Methodist Free Church almanac or this year (1898) records his name as being six times president of the Con- ference.and the portrait of his.wife Hannah Phillips fills the upper &lit hand corner of the sheet. Looking back more than sixty years how vividly the old town of my childhood, Warrington upon the Mersey, returns to me. It was a sleepy old town then, lying upon its broad river, circled round by calm fields and flowery hedgerow's, overlooked by the dark crest of Hill Cliff and the heights of Halton Castle, the Arpley meadows and broad salt marches of Cuerdley lying between. Our main street was Bridge-street, which aecended from the river to the Market Gate, where the latter merged itself in the but ter market. Half way up was Friar's Green, where stood the chapel in which we all assembled on Sundays, and where my grandfather,en ardent disciple of Wesley, frequently preached. Here and there were quaint gabled magpie houses of the seventeenth century. In the midst the famous hostelry of the Red Lion reared its sign aloft, and I used to watch the swift mail coaches dashing up with sounding horns, the guards shining in scarlet and gold. Following these came an endless succession of gigs, post- chaises,vans, and wagons, which crowded the high- way from Manchester to Liverpool, furnishing con- tinual interest to the loungers and gossips at the street corners. Not far off staid the stocke,where I occasionally have seen a lusty toper bear the brunt ' of public detislon. We knew fill our neighbours, high and low, by sight and names, and took a kindly and social in- terest in their proceedings. It seems to me as I re- call past incidents, that the little town abounded in originals of the most varied types. One of the most prominent was a certain so-called Captain Leigh, an old sailor, who often looked in at my grandfather's workshop. He wore a long blue coat with large brass buttons, corduroy knee breeches, white stock- ings, and was ornamented by a pig-tail which I used to twitch, and then vanish round a corner. Many had been 010 Leigh's adventures, and some of them found their way into a-contemporary newspaper or magazine. Perhaps some of sour readers may have read tt em, and can recall the name of the publica- tion. His most terrible experiences were on board a Liverpool shin engaged in the slave trade. Leigh lived to a great ag and he had a novel way of celebrating hie birthday. With two or three par- ticular cronies he used to drive in a cart through the principal streets arreyed in his Sanday clothes. His grave-stone inscribed with his name was reared up in the cart, a barrel of ale occupied another corner, and he treated his aeauaintances to free drinks en route, stooping at intervals to sing "Rule Britannia "and various other nautical ditties. Such small diversions made a town show. We bad no concerts, no penny readings, nor guilds, nor Shakespere societies, and our dim-fronted poorly- lighted shops gave small promise of the rich Cheshire cheese and good farmer; hams and butter

interior, the shavings and sawdust on the floor, and the pulleys in the roof connected with a big wheel crown below. Three of his big sons worked along with him. Masters. sons, and workmen were all pretty much on an equality, and there were plenty of jake, fragments of songs, aild what Pepys calls " good discourse " when the master deemed it best to give things a serious turn. But no oaths were allowed, though sometimes party feeling ran high and Whig and Tory fell fool of each other. In the big grate burned a fire of shavings and log ends. Occasionally, to my great joy, a sort of iron gibbet was let down the big chimney displaying a row of hams, juicy and shinieg and of a rich sienna brown, which had been hung there to be flavoured by the aromatic pine and oak shavings. Never were such collops as those hams yielded ! Many were my visits to the workshops, and it was on one of these occasious that I actually took part, in my small way, in shaping some of the accessories of the famous railway of Stephenson. My grandfather, his proper line of business being slack, took a contract to furnish a number of wooden balls or plugs with iron spikes in them, which were to be used in the construction of the iron road. They were made by means of a steel punch fixed firmly in a wooden handle or head. This punch was placed on a piece of wood, the head was struck smartly with a wooden mallet and a cylindrical peg was produced. Into this: was fixed an iron spike, and it was then ready for use. I persuaded my un'l a to let me try this operation, and to my great glee found that I could produce these wooden pegs with considerable success. And so I may say with truth that I assisted in the con- struction of the Liverpool and Manchester line, the first passenger railway in the world. When I was last in the town of my birth it was to lay in the grave the last of the friends of my youthful days left on the old spot, and it was diffi- cult to believe that it was the same heaven and earth that rejoiced my young soul. We are told that the revolution brought about by the invention of the locomotive is a beneficent one. It may be so; but. I look in vain for that life of plain living and high thinking of which my grandparents were an example. The sky is hidden in smoke; the bright river of my youth is loaded with filth from the teeming cities of ineustry ; and the friendly faces and familiar voice,—they too are gone! As Walter von der Vogelweide sings—

in response to Mr. Price's signal, a hansom drew up to the kerbstone, just outside the Princess Delilah's residence, and at the same time two men sauntered up to the doorway. Whatever might be the Princess's desire to display her charms to the best advantage under all circumstances, she did not spend much time in dressing, and did not keep the hansom waiting above five minutes ere she presented herself, ready for departure. Mr. Price was in a state of ever-increasing bewilderment; for his infatuation for this beautiful woman was fighting with the persistently obtruding suspicion that she was a criminal of the deepest dye, or, at all events, the accomplice of criminals. On seeing how resplendently lovely she looked, a recollection of his own sartorial deficiencies struck the fastidious American; and he glanced in dismay at his crumpled shirt-front and evening dress, which his light,dustcoat was scarcely able to hide. For the first time also he discovered that he was minus a good many of the articles he had worn when he came to this house on the previous night. "Good Heavens!" he thought. "Surely the necklace isn't gone!" But an examination of his pockets proved that it was no longer in his possession; and his mind was in an extraordinary turmoil of conflicting emotions as he hastily advanced to meet the Princess, who was, however, in so pre-occupied a mood that the only notice she took of him was to respond to the .Testure which indicated the waiting hansom. He .vas about to assist her into it, when a curious feeling of repulsion seized him, and the hand which would have been held out to her dropped limply by his side.

"She must be speaking the truth," was the thought which flashed through his mind; "and when she came after me last night, it was only to make sure that I had not escaped from the trap to which she had sent me If robbery was the object, it appears to have been successful; and I shall present a fine figure at Scotland Yard. But, never mind, if she can go through with it, I will." The Princess had noticed his instinctive action, and after springing into the hansom without his assistance, she turned upon him a face vThich was blazing with scorn. But she uttered not a word to him, nor did she speak again until the short drive to Scotland Yard was completed. Mr. Price was in such a chaos of bewilderment that he failed"to see that he and his companion were being rigorously shadowed by the two men who were on duty in Mrs. Rizzen-Quicke's interests. At present all that they had to do was to guard against the Princess Delilah's escape. But they were doing that so effectually that her criminal career was certain to have reached its limit, even if wild jealousy had not prompted her to the full confession she was about to make.

Her destination was somewhat of a surprise to the detectives; but it did not take them off their guard, and they followed her to the door of the room in which she was presently interviewed by an inspector, who took i.lown her statements as methodically as if he were not face to face with the greatest professional sensation of his life. Stripped of its interpolations, this is the gist of what the Princess Delilah had to reveal,:

"I- was born in Courland twenty-nin't years ago, at the castle of my uncle, Prince Nicholas of Cour- land, to whom my -mother had returned after the death of her husband, Prince Carlos of Mitylene, whom she only survived three years. She brought with her her only son, and we were, educated together. But my uncle had a fancy for making me his heiress ; and, with , this end in view, he obtained a permit from the Czar to enable me to succeed to his title. Therefore, although I was born a Princess of.Alitylene; I have 'always been known as the Princess of Courland ; and after my brother and I grew up, we found that it suited our plans better to appear to be unrelated to each other. When I was nine years old, the Duke and Duchess of Hapsburg Solltesein came to visit my uncle. briuging with them their only son, Otto, who was my senior by ten years. In spite of our high- sounding titles, we were none of us possessed of wealth enough to gratify our luxurious tastes; and after the death of my uncle, it did not take my brother and me long to dissipate ,the fortune he had left me.

"In being thus extravagant, we .were not uneasy about the Suture, .for both Carlos and I had gifts which we knew we should be able to turn to good account. He had a passion for science, and the abstrusities of electrical engineering were child's play to him. As an inventor, he had a great future before him, and I—well, I knew that I was beautiful, and I only needed to exercise my witcheries to wheedle fortunes out of my admirers. But to live strictly on these lines was too tame an existence for us; and after we began to see a great deal of our friend Otto, whose parents were now Adead, we struck up a partnership which has so far been as phenomenally successful as it has been daring. "It has been my task to win the hearts of rich men, and if they shewed no disposition to beggar themselves for love's sake, to entice them into the tiger's den, to which I will presently conduct you. In this way about a dozen wealthy men have been disposed of in London alone; but as the majority of them were birds of passage, their informal dis- appearances were attributed either to eccentricity or to lack of courtesy on their part. With some of the later victims it has been different, for there were plenty of people in London who were interested in them; and we had almost decided to seek a fresh field for our work, as it could no longer be pursued here with impunity. "It will be news to a great many people to learn that the Princess Delilah of Courland and Madame Celia,' the wonderful clairvoyante, are one and the same person. As a Princess I gathered ts fund of information coacerning the feather- ,ained society folks who came to consult Madame ' and who never dreamt that the sorceress who concealed her fair skin and blonde hair by a marvellously life-like mask and a purple-black wig, . was the beautiful Princess.

"One of my more recent devotees was Mr. John Mills, who had a hobby for collecting precious stones, and who had the still greater peculiarity of keeping his hobby secret from his friends. He gave the star-sapphire to Mrs. Rizzen-Quicke. But, contrary to his statement, it was by no means the only gem he possessed at the time; and it remained for me to worm his secret out of him. After I made this discovery, Mr. Mills had but a short career; and it was in connection.with his disappear- ance that I found it necessary to give up the use of my favourite :flowers. I heard accidentally that when he was last seen by the porter of the mansions in which he lived,' there was a mark on his coat caused by the adhesion of the pollen of a flower .similar to some that he ' had brought home with him. Luckily, these flowers, which I had given to Mr. Mills, had been thrown away before they could be used as a clue by a certain amateur detective, who has busied himself over the affair. But the

"This letter was written while young Rizzen- Quicke was labouring under hypnotic compulsion, as were also the signatures of certain cheques which had been cashed for our benefit from time to time. I have no doubt, in his normal condition, he would have been horrified at the idea of stealing anything at all, much less the most valuable gem his mother had. Of course, the letter in which he said that he was going on a yachting tour was as far from the mark as were many others which have puzzled the police. "Although I got a fright when I inspected the piece of blotting-paper, and realised what a very narrow escape I had had, I soon recovered my composure, and invented a plan which was calculated to dispose effectually of those who dis- trusted us. "An enormous amount of gems have come into my possession during the last ten years, and after they have been removed from their settings by my brother, they have generally been sold as part of our ancestral inheritance. Sometimes, when watching other women, I have thought that if I could only be as innocent and care-free as they were, I would give all I possessed. But these ideas have invariably been banished again by the intoxication of complete success. "My love has always been given to the Duke of Hapsburg-Solltesein. But I have, nevertheless, revelled in the adulation of other men, and their worship has been the incense which has urged me to still more merciless conquests. And now all this is to come to an end, because my confederate, • after knowing me for twenty years, is still unable to read my character. He thinks that he can befool me and throw me on one side, and he has even sneered about me to another woman! This is an unpardonable offence, and it is this that has induced me to unravel the whole of the mysteries which have defied elucidatimi until now. "One word more.. Mr. Samuel Mosenthal, although he professed to be wildly in love with me, was a very difficult man to deal with, for he worshipped Mammon still more than he worshipped Cupid, and no amount of flattery or bewitchment 'could cause him to lose his head. Indeed, I would have given him up as a failure, had he not been too dangeruos a man to be allowed to escape. He, the Duke, and my brother, had promoted a big syndicate, and although their names did not appear in the prospectus, he trusted them so completely that if they could only have got rid of him, they would have had no difficulty in appropriating the many thousands of pounds which were sent in by eager sharebuyers. But as I have already said, I found him an obstinate subject to deal with, and on the occasion of his last visit to my house, 'he even failed to yield to the Duke's hypnotic powers. As it was not desirable to allow him to go at large again, a struggle became necessary, and it was during this struggle that his watch-chain was broken. But I never dreamt that part of it had been left behind when he was subsequently removed to—the tiger's den. You shall know what that den is like soon. "My brother lives in the next street to me, and his house and mine stand almost back to back. Having plenty of money, and being in the habit of paying liberally for any services that are rendered to us, we are always able to secure whatever co- operation we need, and our subordinates are invariably Russians or Greeks, who are naturally antagonistic to English people, while, at the same time, they revere us as the last representatives of a long line of Sings, whose grandeur had faded before that of the British dynasty had begun. In their eyes we are entitled to exercise 'the Divine Right of Kings,' to do whatever pleases them, and as they believe that 'Kings can do no wrong, they never hesitated to do our bidding, so they must not be held responsible for their obedience. "To continue. These servitors of ours have, by dint of great labour, constructed a subterranean connection between the house in which I reside and the house which is rented by my brother. This passage is carefully walled in and flagged, and my brother has himself fitted it with electric light, so that it may be available for our use at any time of the night or day. The precautions we have taken are so great, and the way of communciation is so cleverly hidden, that we have enjoyed absolute immunity from detection, and it is doubtful if all the resources of Scotland Yard could have success- fully unearthed our secret without my aid., But come, I expect that my accomplices, as you would call them, will soon arrive at my brother's house with some prisoners, who are not intended to leave it again, and if you want to save their lives you have no time to lose."

While the Princess Delilah had been uttering this strange confession, her auditors were spell- bound with amazement. The story she had told them implied that she was an accessory, if not a principal, in a wholesale series of murders. Yet she spoke with such little evidence of horror or remorse that. it was difficult to believe her state. ments. On the other hand, if she spoke the truth, every moment was of the most vital importance, and in an incredibly short space of time the Princess, Mr. Priee, the • inspector, and two plain-clothes policemen, were driving back to the house which had been described as the entrance to a tigoeo slaa.

CHAPTER, XXVL

" THE REVELATOIN.

"This way," said the Princess Delilah, whose erstwhile fury had calmed down into a deadly implacability under the influence of which , she looked more satanically beautiful than ever. "There are plenty of you to ensure your mutual safety, and though I am going to lead you into one of the most hellish traps that scientiiic genius has ever invented, I give you the word of a Delilah that no harm shall come to you."

The promise thus given did not prove very re- assuring, for those who heard it were not too favourably impressed by the speaker's sense of honour. But there was no faltering from the task which had been undertaken, and treachery was guarded against by placing an officer at the entrance to the subterranean passage. The said entrance was capitally adapted for eluding observation, for it was ostensibly the door of a Wardrobe, which only the initiated possessed means of opening. • Passing through this wardrobe the traitorous Princess led Mr. Price and the officers of the law down a steep flight of stone steps, along a smoothly.' floored corridor, which had nothing very contraband-looking about it, after she had turned the electric light on, and to the foot of another flight of steps. Then she cautioned silence, and listened for a few moments ere she ventured further.

"We are in time," she whispered, and a look of diabolical satisfaction, which overspread her face as she spoke, produced a strong . effect upon at least two of her companions. "My God! How can I ever have thought ,that I loved this fiend!" was Mr. Price's reflection.

"Of course, the woman is crazy, downright crazy," thought the inspector. Nor was he far wrong. But he had yet to learn what ghastly method there was in her madness, and there was not a man there who did not recoil in awful horror when she opened a door close to the foot of the stairs and turned the electric light upon the scene within. The chamber, thus revealed, was narrow and long, and it had evidently been originally intended for an ordinary cellar, until devilish ingenuity suggested another use for it. Round the walls were built a series of niches, nearly all of which were occupied by a recumbent figure, ivhich„ at first sight, looked as though it were carved out of marble. But he was too quick for them. The knowledge that he had been betrayed, and that all was now over with him flashed through his mind like lightning,' and his resolve to be his. own executioner was equally swift.. Before his would-be:captors could realise what was happening, he had struck them' bpth aside, had sprung into the fatal chair, and had suffered the fate to which he had mercilessly consigned so many others. !` The Prince,' shouted Mr. Price. him escape us, t 00.7, But the warning came too late, for both the Prince and Princess had.vanished. What became of them afterwards is ,not known; but they must, have had plenty of help from their minor accomplices, and they must have been very cleverly disguised to be able to elude the search which was made for them. It is hardly likely that they remained in England long. But wherever they may be they are sure to be making a perverted use of the advantages which Nature has bestowed upon them, and it is to be hoped that their oileer is near its close. When it was realised that the Princess Delilah had once more justified her name by befooling the people to whom she had confessed all her wicked- ness, the chagrin which was felt was almost enough to outbalance the triumph of discovery. But this feeling soon wore away, for, after , all, great things • had been achieved. The kidna-pping mysteries were mysteries no longer. Fresh murders had been prevented, and the ringleader of the gang was dead. A recurrence of the same sort of crime was practically impossible, for Delilah's witchery had not always been potent enough with- out the aid of the Duke's mesmeric power, and happily, he could not be replaced by another accomplice of equal ability. His death lifted the spell under which his latest subjects were labouring, and one can easily imagine the excitement which reigned when their narrow escape was explained to them.

Poor Mr. llizzen-Quicke was almost heart- broken at the confirmation of his worst ' fears respecting his son. But when he would have visited the cellar, to gaze upon his remains, the hearthstone was found to be immovable, and com- munication had afterwar::,.; to be effected throuzla

In short, it wadi acknowledged all round that even in her madness the Princess Delilah had proved herself to be a. more cunning individual than the authorised representatives of the law. , " I

A few years have passed since the _disappearance of the Princess Delilah, and the circumstances attending it have now become ancient history. But some of the friends whom we have met deserve e• parting word of mention. Lord Alfred and Polly are happily married, and Mr. and Mrs. Rizzen- Quicke's sorrow for their son is chastened by the knowledge that all is well with their daughter and her husband and children. . _

Lady Flora Vavasonr-PomerOy has found a. congenial helpmate, and two great friends of hers are Mr. and Mrs. Price, the latter being a lady who was formerly known as the Countess Sforza.

Neither the Countess's necklace nor Mrs. Rizzen= Quicke's star-sapphire ever reverted to their right- ful owners. But neither one nor the other lamented this fact, because they would only have served as painful reminders of the infamous career, of the Princess Delilah and her associates.

to be found within. But then there were no flaring gin vaults,nor hordes of male and female noodlums, dirty and blasphemous; thrift, decency, and re- straint pervaded saciety,and a wholesome reverence for one's "betters."

The pride and glory of our town, along with its eld church nod its mail coaches and the red brick houses of the gentry, was the river, the world-re- nowned Mersey. I used to watch it foaming and trembling under the bridge near which the old quay packet used to start daily for Manchester. Another great sour-ce of interest were the lock and the weir. In the seasen you could see the big salmon lespince and there I once beheld a lordly sturgeon captured. On the river wall near the bridge you would see on most fine days a row of anglers, and on the water's edge I and my eldest brother used to catch "stock- ing-needles" (small eels), now and then tumbling in and being carried away. I had once a narrow escape of being droWned under the arch of the bridge, but I soon learned to swim, seeming to take to the water from instinct. Another absorbing de- light was to watch the tMerm,-n draw in their nets, all rolling and flashing silver,loaded with sparlings. There was no end to the riverside delights of Old Warrington sixty-five years ego ! What a place it was for boya ! I used to go to school to EEPep me out of danger, but they gave me very few lessons; the three Ws being the limit, of instruction. The river and the fields were my school, and the summer skies, then undimmed by the hideous smoke voided from the hundred chim- neys of the Warrington of to-day. Great was our excitement when the river was in flood after long rains in the Cheshire and Derbyshire hills. It swept down with resistless force from Wilderspool, past Runcorn, Hale,. and Frodsham, turning Arnley meadows and the salt marshes into a vast lake. The sea-gulls came up in thousands and whitened the muddy shores, rising in clouris when a gun was fired. It was great excitement to punt about in this vast lake, and rather dangerous, but my uncles were the boatmen, and knew how to avoid the sunk stakes and submerged hedgerows. Being only a few years older than myself we were like playmates. We did not reach home till dusk, muddy, wet, happy,and hungrye=nd how sweet was our sleep after a supper of buttermilk and porridge, under the roof of our childhood. . An avenue of noble elms then shaded the way to Arnley meadows. They have been long since poisoned by the fumes of chemical works, and only one or two skeleton trunks remain. There I used to wander for hours, losing myslf among fields of wheat and barley, and now and then coming upon a hidden pool overhung by alder trees and willows where our nurse-girl told me dwelt a certain Jenny Greenteeth who would seize me by the hair and drag me down if I went there in the dusk. And these fair scenes have been defaced cr blotted out altogether, and it le many decades since the salmon ceased to ascend the river and the fishermen ta ply their nets.

But I have wandered far from the theme with which I began these scribblings, and must hark back to the subject with which I started. My maternal grandfather, Peter Phillips, Wesleyan preacher and cabinetmaker, was a man of marked originality. According to his views of religion o man should, like St. Paul, work for his bread during the day and preach the gospel without fee. Acting on. these principles he never "got on '' in a worldly point of view while his brother John rose to high esteem as a paid minister, and even counted a provincial mayor among hiiuff- spring. A portrait of my grandfather, made by me, is still extant. He was a sturdy man of patriarchal aspect, serious of speech, grave-eyed, and wore a suit of Quaker garb, an ample-skirted coat, white kerchief, knee-breeches, and blue knitted stockings, with low shoes. He was widely known among the primitive Methodists and laboured hard every Sabbath, and, in spare hour., among the weavers and the colliers arid in the rural district that lay for twenty miles round Warrington. He walked many thousand miles in the course of his long life, and was highly esteemed and sought out in his humble dwelling by such men as Adam Clarke, Lorenzo Dow, and Wilson Mackay. He had built himself a workshop not far from the Red Lion, and it was my delight to go up into the loft and watch the various processes going on. I remember well the pic7ure..que

PRINCESS DELILAH.

By MRS. GEORGE CORBETT, Author of "When the Sea Gives up its Dead,') "The Prince Physician," "The Vindicatiort of Olive Trevethick," "The Star of Yukon,' "The Adventures of an Ugly Girl," "Deb 06, MaHy's," "A Fair Blockade Runner," &c.

CHAPTER XXV.

DELIL AM ' S CONFESSION.

The Princess Delilah's serving woman was greatly distressed at the turn affairs were taking, and she implored her mistress to consider what she was •-,njna,

will ruin us all, madame," she said, "if you communicate with the police."

"It will not ruin you or anyone whom I have employed," was the firm reply, "for, in view of a possible collapse of our plans, I have provided for you all. In the top drawer of my secretaire you will find envelopes, addressed to yourself and the others. Keep your own, and give the rest to the people for whom they are intended. You will all find yourselves well-to-do, and you had better go back to your own country, to spend the rest of your days there. Mr. Price, I want you to go to Scotland Yard with me. Will you secure a hansom while I get myself ready to go out? Serano, give me the hat which came home yesterday—the one with the white plumes. And I will wear the white serge costume." "There is an instance of the ruling passion for you," thought Mr. Price. "'The woman is mad, that's certain, and she talks like a tragedy queen. Yet she bestows careful thought upon the costume in which she is to pose before the authorities of Scotland Yard! I wonder if it is true that she knows everything about the people she mentioned. She promises to let me see all that is left of them; and yet almost in the same breath she tells me that there is life to save. I cannot make head or tail of it. But I will keep the Princess Delilah in sight until the police know a trifle more than they do now.

circumstance was a lesson to me, and I nave banished flowers from my rooms ever since.

—"The star-sapphire, which I boldly wore at opera, had not been bought by me, as I asserted, but was actually the same stone which its real owner, Mrs. Rizzen-Quicke, believed to be reposing safely in its case. It was brought to me by Mr. Edward Rizzen-Quicke. He was a silly boy enough; but he must not be blamed for the theft, for he was labouring under mental compulsion. "The Duke of Hapsburg Solltesein is a hypnotist, and by means of psychological suggestion, he compels people to obey his wishes, even to the extent of going to their bank and withdrawing large sums of money in gold. In all cases where this manceuvre has been successful, the fate of the subject has been sealed; and our arrangements have always been so cunningly made that nobody connected the dis- appearances with us until Lord Alfred Vavasour- Pomeroy got on our track. He and Edward Rizzen-Quicke's father came to consult Madame Celia, bringing with them certain articles which Droved that the officious young lord was working in the right direction. I was staggered for a moment when I saw these articles, for they were very incriminatory; and if the blotting - paper had revealed every word that was written in the note, it would certainly have given an infallible clue to the whereabouts of the star-sapphire. As nearly as I can recollect, the note ran thus: "Dearest Delilah, —Your commands shall be obeyed. I long to prove my devotion to the Goddess of Beauty. You shall have the star- sapphire, and I rely upon your promises.—Your devoted TEDDY. .

===== 0002601_18990407_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
Convince ine norrinea uenoiaers tnaL they were gazing upon corpses, not upon carven marble. "These, gentlemen, are the proofs of what I and my colleagues can do," resumed the Princess noting with a weird kind of satisfaction the petrifying effect which the sight of the wax-like bodies had upon the people to whom she spoke. "We used our subjects until they had served our purpose, but, as you see, there is nothing revolting about our manner of treatment. They did not suffer a moment, and after death they were pre- served from decay by a method which is my brother's invention, and of which he is very proud. I dare- say you are wondering how it is that none of the bodies bear traces of violence. It is easily explained. In a room to which I will conduct you presently, there is a certain chair, in which our intended victims are induced to seat them- selves at the will of the Duke of HapsburgSolltesein. He has made animal magnetism du, special study of his life, even as electricity is the chosen science of my brother. The one hypnotises superfluous persons and suggests that they seat themselves in the fatal chair, which is, in reality, a clever electrical apparatus, of which my brother is the inventor. At the right moment the electric current is turned on, and in a second the occupant of the chair is dead. Then a portion of the carpet is raised, the hearthstone is made to revolve on pivots, and the body is lowered into the cellar. Then it is laid on one of these slabs, and receives subcutaneous injections of a fluid which has the property of turning flesh into the semblance of marble. Decomposition is rendered impossible, and the body gradually hardens into perfect rigidity without losing any of the physical peculiarities which characterised it during life. That lam not making an undue boast of our powers must be quite evident to you. I fascinated most of our victims, the Duke hypnotised them, the Prince turned them into statues, and we all plundered them. There you have the case in a nutshell. If the Duke had not played me false, no doubt there would have been a good many more 'mysterious disappearances.' But he little knew whom or what he was slighting, and our triple partnership is at an end." While the Princess had been speaking, Mr. Price and the Officials had overcome their first horror, and had assured themselves of the identity of several of the rigid figures which lay upon tho slabs. Recognition was rife amongst them. Then* were eight bodies altogether, among them being John Mills, Teddy Rizzen-Quicke, and Samuel Mosenthal. Some of the others were recognised by the police. But as they had no special bearing upon the development of this story, it is not neces- sary to enumerate them by name. I On touching the face or hands of any one of them, the sensation imparted was precisely as if cold stone or marble had been touched, and it struck Mr. Price that an invention which could permanently arrest decay would prove an enormous boon to the world, if it could be rightly used by, the right people. ; "H's't I " said the Princess suddenly. "I believe, 4the others are returning! Let us enter the room above before they do, or we shall be too late to save the people whom they expected to bring with them. LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAR NELLIE, If we have a mild early spring, as prophesied, small collet additions for the ahoulders will be generally worn, a cloth collet, trimmed fur, which, made to match a gown, would be most useful. If made in black or green velvet, with fur borders, iji could be worn over any gown. Over a pleated ilkirt of pink crepe de chine is worn a narrowly- arranged tunic, with long graduated points em- broidered with roses of different shades. The bodice, draped transversely, fastens at the side with an embroidered band, and is cut in a square and is sleeveless. Round the shoulders is grace- fully draped a sash trimming of gaze de soie. An- other gown of saffron Liberty satin is cut in Prin- cess shape. Applications of lace wave round the skirt, which is long and sinuous; these are repro.

, duced on the close-fltting bodice round the tidcol- 16tage and among the draped gauze encircling the throat. Perhaps you have noticed that we are running into extremes in sleeves, Once they were too large now they are the opposite. They are put in very high in the armhole, and fall well over the hand. In evening gowns they become small by degrees and beautifully less. Very becoming are the epaulettes of rose leaves, dyed to match the colour of the dress, which taper to a point in the centre, where they widen considerably ; they have to do full duty ; no tulle nor lace accompanies them, but occasionally, while they cover the top of the shoulder, crossing ribbons, covered with small flowers and a gossamer puffing, surround the arm below. It requires a good deal of adjustment to get the collar band high enough and not too high, and it should be higher under the ears than elsewhere, forming an acute point. Then the jacket should open sufficiently at the neck, when the weather permits, for a pretty becoming portion of the vest to be seen, and the dainty gossamer bow.

There are some very fine new fashions coming into vogue just now, especially coat and skirt costumes, which are very pronounced features in spring fashions, and I saw a novel and attrac- tive design one day which was a pearl-grey poplin, the skirt having a band of ivory cloth at the hem, giving the effect of a double skirt. The coat, with fitting back had strapped and stitched seams, and the loose open fronts, with the revers, faced with ivory satin overlaid with cream Irish guipure. Under this is waistcoat and crossed fronts of pale Parma mauve brocade, and plain vest of ivory satin and guipure. Among the coat and skirt costumes E.re some with the new habit backs and short fronts, which &re exceedingly becoming, and have a very smart appearance. One, a very pale beaver-coloured box cloth, is trimmed with silk braid both on the skirt and coat, and the sleeves are scalloped at the wrist and braided round the edges. The coat is lined with pale green glace silk, and is in every respect a very stylish garment.

An exceptionally good bargain will be found in a costume of Venetian, trimmed with silk braiding on the coat and skirt, and with striped satin linings to thc coat. _

Avery pretty costume for young ladies is one of fawn Venetian cloth, with a sacgue back and stitched edges. The coat is Lind through with shot glae6 silk, and the skirt is mounted at the back in the fashionable flat pleats, with little buttons on either side. For a child of ten this is a smart little costume. _ . _ _ _ _

A very simple morning blouse is the tucked shirt or blouse which will still remain fashionable, and many of these are so elaborated with grouped tucks, insertions, and stitched bands that they become positively costly, and quite unnecessarily smart. Where tucks are run on the straight they are comparatively easy to make, and the amateur will have no difficulty in doing so. Your 'lying JENNIE.

"WOMEN'S CHAT."

There is, as usual, a large gathering of the Danish Royal family at Copenharzen, in honour of the King's birthday, aud for the first time a shadow rers on toe reunion. Queen Louise, one of the two central figures in that happy group, is no longer there to welcome her children an I grandchildren beck to the old 'acme and her loss must have been especially keenly felt during the past wePk or two. The town residence of the Danish Royal Family is in the lams square of Copenhagen known as the Arnaben- borg. 'The summer residences are Reseaberg Castle, where the Crown jewels are kept, Frederikseorg Castle situated meat beautifully on an islane in the centre of , a large lake, and the Chateau of Berustorff where the Princess of Wales, the Dowager Empress of Russia, th King of Greece, the Crown Prince of Denmark and toe Duchess of Cueseerland spent their childi.h days. The Amabenborg Palace is most sumptuously fureished. and I moreover a veritable picture gallery of contemporary' royalties. Charlottenlund Castle, the summer residence of the Crown Prince and Crown Princess of Denmark is about two miles distant from Berustorff. It is a charming old fashioned house, strongly recalling a mediaeval French chat-eau. The Princess is enormiusly wealthy. Her jewels are wonderful, and she enjoys the reputation of being the tallest Princess in Europe. She has four EonS arid four dauehters, one of the former,Princa Carl, being the husband of our Princess Mated of Wales. The Lady Warwick Hostel„ Reading, established for the purpose of training women in dairying, poultry- raising, bee keeping, and practical horticulture, Ncla fair to be a huge success. Altnough so recently opened as December 10:h, there are but a few vacancies, and with the full complement of twenty-seven students, no endowment will be necessary. The course of training extends over two years, though arrangements can be made for short courses on special s 'ejects. The inclu- sive fees am6unt to from £5O to £6O a year, while the higher tees of £BO to £l2O, procure the advantage of a "study bedroom." As to the prospects open to students On Paving, it has been demonstratei that a moderate livelihood can be made in the lighter branches of agri- culture, while our foreign trade proves that the demand for the class of goods it is proposed to produce far out- runs the home supply. Tnere is also an increasing in- quiry for women managers for large dairies, awl for thoroughly competent lecturers and demonstrators. It has been estimated that in 1897, eggs to the value of £812,297 and poultry to the value of £lB6 825, were imported from Russia alone. And the Rus,ien contri- bution to the British market is really incon- siderable in comparison with those of France and Denmark, making it the more inconceiv- able that we do not turn our attention to wards supplyinge the enormous demand by home pro- duction. Some of the Russian produce is brought 800 to 1,000 miles frem the interior to the Baltic seaboard. and has to undergo a further sea journey of seven or eight days before the British market is reached. More- over Russian eggs are, even in their pristine freshness, much smaller and inferior in quality to the home pro- duced article and it is obvious this long journey by rail and sea must make the inferiority all the more pro- nounced.

One of the oldest and most universal of all Easter customs, is that associated with Easter eggs. It was in the first instance derived from the heathen spring festi- val, when eggs were given typifying the revivification of nature. Originally the eggs appear to have been taken on the morning of Easter to the priest, who blessed them, they were then carried home, and either par- taken of at a special meal, or given to relations and friends. Among members of the Greek church, the cus- tom retains its religious significance, aud is observed with much ceremony. A crop of spring novelties are now with us, hut the wise will refrain from making any important purchases until spring is a little more advanced, for to buy a new coat or gown in April, with a view to wearing it through the season, and t 3 find in May that it is a style rejected by the best dressed, is to court discontent and consequent financial embarrassment. Things Irish are still to the fora—lrish frieze enjoys immense vogue for tailor mades, and Irish lace is very fashionable for trimming dresses. Scotch tweeds too, abound and prove eelightful wear at this especially treacherous season of the year. Few people seem to be aware chat some of the most beautiful upholstering silks in the world are made in England. And the cost is not so great as would be that of foreign silks of the same sort, most of which Come to the British market heavily weighted with no less sub- stantial a material than tin. The manufacture of English silks has had its decadent period, but it is now enjoying an agreeable and prosperous renaissance, which our countrywomen might lift to the height of a glorious triumph if they would, and without suffering, but rather benefitting in purse and person. The most up-to-date mackintosh is the long coat shape double breasted, and fastening with huge pearl buttons, the fullness at the back setting neatly unaer the pointed yoke. Other designs show the plain" sac back," or the fullness confined at the waist by a stitched strap. There are two kinds of sleeves, the plain" coat." and the one commonly described as the "bishop." The latter is really the most comfortable. If short ccats are pre- ferred, there are those dainty little garments, reaching just below the hips, piped with silk in some contrasting colour, with collar facings to match. The most popular hose supporter fastens round the waiac with a satin band that is narrow in the front and back, but comes down well over the hips. One piece of narrow silk elastic is met half way down by two others, and at the end of each are rubber buttons and metal clamps to catch the hosiery. The rubber buttons have many advantages over the old fashioned metal ones that have been in use for years. In the first place the rounded rib on the holding edges of the metal loop, and the robber button prevents all possibility of wear on the stocking by chafing, and the rubber button is rigid enough to hold its position, and still elastic enough to adjust itself automatically to the finest lisle thread or silk stocking as well as to the heavier cotton or woolen hose.

OMELET SOUFFLE.—Take four eggs, four ounces of sugar, the grated rind of half a lemon, and two ounces of butter. Separate the yolks from the whites, beat the former, add to them the sugar and letton rind. Whisk the whites well, and beat in. Melt the butter in a small frying pan, pour in the mixture, and stir over a clear fire until the omelet sets. Turn on to a hot dish, fold in two, and serve immediately. It should rise very much, and be served very quickly before It can go down. " MADGE."

ENVELOPES 2s. P R 11000.

JOHN WALKER AITD CO., ARTISTIC PRIN'PERS

EX MINER OFFICE, WA r2TtINGTON,

THE 1-41XAM1'TEE, FM DAY APRIL 7, 1899. TIE ha dr 31. T. IO Als PATEL HALL. Encouraged by his suceess last year Mr. W. J. Hooper has again ventured to hold a second trades' and industrial exhibition in Parr Hall. The opening ceremony was performed on Saturday afternoon in the presence of a goo 1 number of visitors. Some fifty exhibits have been crowded within the spacious area °lithe hall,hut they can hardly be said to adequa- tely represent the industrial resources of the town. Very few trades are to be seen at work and some of the chief industries are entirely unrepresented. But notwithstanding these drawbacks, which are to some extent accounted for by the lack of space. an interesting exhibition has been got together,and Mr. Hooper is to be congratulated upon his enter- prise. Many of the old exhibitors are again repre- sented and several new ones are included. A num- ber of Warrington tradesmen are well to the fore and their displays give evidence of careful and painstaking work. Descriptions of the exhibits will be found appended. In addition to the exhibits, the National Ladies Orchestra discourses delightful music both afternoon and evening, and other attractions are provided in the form of the kallas- cope, mutascope, the cycloscope, the incubator, the palmist and X rays demonstrations. Mr. E. H. Mond, is the palmist, and he has had a large num- ber of clients at his stand who have had their hands " practically" explained. A centre of at- traction has been the fisn pond, described as " the latest electrical novelty." The hall is again lighted with electricity, the contract on this occasion hav- ing been let to the Warrington and District Electric Light and Power Company, Limited. Adequate arrangements have been made for the "inner man." Tea, coffee and refreshment rooms have been provided, and they are again under the direction of the representatives of the Park Bakery, Sankey-street, which it may be stated is fitted with the latest machinery and ovens for the production of bread and confectionery on the most cleans and hygienic principles, and is open to visitors to the exhibition for inspection. Being the most modern and up-to-date bakery in the district, it is well worth a visit. Mr. Horton is superintending the arrangements for the tea rooms which are taste- fully set out, and in every way suited for the pur- pose. Refreshments of a different description are to be obtained at the Exhibition bar which is under the capable management of Messrs. J. Shillcock and P. 11. Lake. During the week the attendance has been very satisfactory, and there is every likelihood of the exhibition being another financial success. THE OPEMNG CEREUONY. There was a fairly-large attendance at the opening ceremony. In the absence of the Mayor (Alderman Wright) the Rector was asked to presi le, and he was accompanied on the platform by the Rev A. N. Banerjel, cathedral missionary from Calcutta; Councillors Bennett, John Roberts, and Ellin and Mr. Andrew Rathbone. At the outset Mr. Hooper read a leler from the Mayor, who apologised for hie Inability to be present owing to his absenc3 in the north. The Mayoress had that day received the following telegram from. the Mayor: "Carlisle. To Mayoress. Give Hooper kind regards, and wish all success. Am getting better."' Pro- ceeding to refer to the exhibition, Mr. Hooper said it would be conducted on precisely the same lines as the previous one. On that occasion he promised that a cer- tain part of the proceeds should be given to the War- rington and District Nursing Association; this year he had decided that one half of the money derived from the sale of the season tickets should ba devoted to the funds of the Infirmary and Dispensary. He was pleased to state that up to the present time double the number of tickets had been sold compared with the last exhibi- tion, and he hoped to 130 able to hand .over a consider- able sum to a deserving institution to show his interest in all that took place in Warrington. (Applause.) Al- though he was not a Warringtonian he was born within 20 miles of it. He bad offered a cash prize for the best essay on" How to improve the trade of Warrington." Mr. Councillor Bennett—and he did not know anyone who took a deeper interest in the welfare of Warrington —had kindly consented to act I% adjudicator. The essay was confined to 500 words, and the winner was Mr. J. C. Harrington, in the employ of Messrs. John L. Fleming and Company, Limited, Warrington. Mr. HOOPER then read the essay, which was as fol- lows :—" In dealing with this subject it is necessary to treat it in its broader aspects : the trades of Warring- ton are so varied and multitudinous that it is impos- sible to specialize in the space at my disposal. The con- struction of docks on the Ship Canal, and the comple- tion of the East Coast Railway might materially increase the town's prosperity, but as neither of these undertak- ings are likely to be attempted for some time to come, and as it is difficult to estimate new to what extent they would benefit our industries. Id 3 not propose dealing further with them. I will now pass on to a scheme, the means of carrying out which are at hand, land which would considerably in.:mese the volume of our local trade. We have its town a Chamber of Com- inerce, through whose exertions much has already been done to promote local commercial interests ; unfortun- ately, however, the Chamber is not as representative as it might be, and as it doubtless would be if every menu- fazturer recognized its value. I should recommend that its objects should be clearly set forth, and an invitation extended to every firm in the neighbourhood to join. I need scarcely point out the immense influence which a really representative body would have; particularly would this be so in dealing with the railway companies, who would display greater eagerness in meeting them in the matter of rates, Sto., than they now do when dealing with individual firms. If the enlarged Chamber could but bring the carrying companies to a more reasonable frame of mind, it would have amply justified its existence ; for the lower the rates from Warrington the greater the advantage over competing centres of industry. The Chamber would be able to exert great influence with the local authorities to provide improved facilities for the development of trade. Two objects that might be attained—the improvement ol the river, se that it should be always navigable by vessels of 200 tons, and the construction of a public wharf —would tend materi- ally to extend the town's trade. In the development of foreign trade, the Chamber might be very useful ; it might establish an office for the translation of members' circulars, price lists, &c:, into any of the chief foreign languages, and also reduce their quotations to their metrical equivalents. English manufacturers are very conservative, and still persist in conducting their foreign business in their own way,and in their own tongue ; our consuls are continually pointing out the folly of this, as the foreigner naturally gives more heed to a missive couched in familiar terms, than he doss to one in a language with which he is only imperfectly acuainted• Hence, England often misses orders through want of a little thought and enterprise. I regret I have not space to deal with other points, or to better illustrate those named, but I trust I have shown how, with little effort, the trade of Warrington might be greatly improved. (Applause.) Mr. HOOPER, continuing his remarks, said the present exhibition gave the people of Warrington an opportunity of inspecting the manufactures of Warrington, and he appealed to the ladies, who neuld see everything neces- sary to make till home comforteble in the Warrington shops—the goods being as cheap, if not cheaper than in other towns—to spend their money in Warrington. If it was good enough for the men to earn their money in Warrington it was good enough that their wives should spend it there. (Hear, hear, and applause.) Be had offered a gold medal for the best display in the exhibi- tion irrespective of trade. There were some trades in connection with which a stall could not be m ids to look pretty, but he and others who had gone round the exhi- bition had no hesitation in deciding—although he had no wish to detract from the other stalls—that the best for decoration was that belonging to Messrs. Crosfield. The gentleman who had been in charge of the decoration of that stall was not present. His name was Mr. Heath and he congratulated the firm on having such a good representative. (Applause.) The RECTOR, in declaring the exhibition open, said there seemed to be strong and excellent reasons for the exhibition. First of all it stimulated invention. They in England were not supposed to be so inventive as their cousins across the Atlantic, still he thought they had done their share, and there were some notable inven- tions of which Englishmen deserved the credit. Secondly, the exhibition encouraged trade and also secured to them good work. He had travelled a good deal at times on the continent, and lately in foreign ' countries, and wherever he had gone he had been much struck with the excellence of English work. It might not always be so artistic, but it was always thoroughly sound and good. Further, such an exhibition facilitated business. That was a point about which Mr. Hooper thought much. The exhibits were a gathered together and ladies and gentlemen, particularly those engaged in business, could compare and judge for themsselves, so that it helped them very much in their own business. And, also the exhibition wou!d be a benefit to a local institution of great value to them all, as one half of the profits derived from the season tickets was to be devoted to the funds orthe Infirmary, and the public should ap- preciate that generosity very much. Lastly an exhibi- tion of this character provided enjoyment and amuse- ment for them all, and after such a season as Lent, through which he and others had been passing, they would all look forward with pleasure to a month of such enjoyment as they would receive hem an inspection of the exhibits. (Applause.) Councillor BENNETT proposed a vote of thanks tn the Rector, and remarked that he was always ready to assist in any good work in the town, and he was cnr- taints/ assisting in a distinctly good work in opening an industrial exhibition. When Mr. Flnoper suggested that the Rector should do so Mr. Willis demurred to some extent, and said he did Rot know whether it was quite the proper thing for a minister of religion, and that the duty °milt to devolve upon someone intimately connec- ted with commerce. He (the speaker) did not know whether commerce and religion were as closely Connec- ted as they ought to be, but,the more el- •?ly they could

associate commerce with the verities of religion,the better for religion and the better for cornmerce.The Recror had toll them in his ipeech something about our competition on the continent; the defect he had nanaea could, to some extent be got over and amended by exhibitions like the present, and it was bvident that although our manufactures could beat smile of the Continental indus- tries they were sometimes lacking from the artistic point of view. They were, however, doing their best to cultivate the artistic talent, and during the last few months they had had an interesting and successful art exhibition in Warrington, and if they could get the idea of commerce, art and religion, and make them all work together, their industrial supremacy would be assured. (Hear. hear.) Alluding t) the suggestion of Mr. Haver that a wharf should be provided on the Ship Canal, Mr. Bennett said that it had already been provided, but there were no facilities in connection with it. The tradesmen and manufacturers of Warrington up to now had not shown any great anxiety to utilise it. He theught they were making a great mistake.(Hear.hear.) Mr. RATHBONS sec )nded• the motion and -remarked that Canon Willis was extending his usefulness in corn. jog forward as he had done that afternoon and opening the exhibition.

Councillor Jowl ROBS/ITS, in supporting it thanked Mr Hooper on behalf of the Workingmen's Committee for his kindness in devoting half of the proceeds of the sale of the season tickets to the Infirmary. That insti- tution was a great boon to the town; it bad done great good, and was likely to do more good in the future. He had had some experience of it during the past 20 years, and he was pleased to know that it was well managed and that the patients were well treated. He hoped Mr. Hooper would be able to hand over A good round sum. (Hear, hear.) The motion was heartily agreed to. _ _ _ _

The RECTOR in reply, said he quite agreed with Mr. Bennett about the close connection there should be be. tween religion and business. His hesitation abut taking the chlir arose from natural diffidence—from his desire that the bast man should be present to do his utm9st for the exhibition. (tipplause.) The opening proceedings concluded by the band play- ing the National Anthem. THE EXHIBITS.

One of the largest and most comprehensive exhibits is undoubtedly that of Mr. J. S, GARLASD, 51, 53, and 55, Bridge-street, Furnishing Ironmonger, Cede Facto”. Ste. This old established firm occupies stands No 10, 11 and 12,and their exhibits have attracted much attention. The stands looked particularly pleasing, especially at night when brilliantly illuminated with the electric light. Foremost among the exhibits are noticed the world re- uownei Premier Cycles, built of their patent Helical tube which is asserted to be fifty per cent stronger than the ordinary plain tube, of which other cycles are made. Another feature of the Premier Cycle is a ball retaining cup which enables the balls and bearing cone to be re- moved from the bottom bracket or wheel hubs altogether thus obviating the annoyance of all the balls falling out, as soon as the discs are removed. The New Rapid anl Bayliss Thomas" Excelsior" Cycles are also on view, the valient feature of the New Rapid being the celebrated I rrue Tangent wheel, which is universally acknowledged ,to be the strongest and most rigid wheel made. A lead- ing line in ladies and gents machines was also shown I viz: "The Iroleshill" at ten guineas an up to date / Coventry made machine of sterling quality and finish. For a cheap and good machine we have seen no better than the "Wearwell" which was shown in both Ladies and Gents at £7 15s. Altogether some 20 machines were exhibited and those desiring a new mount cannot do better than inspect these. Turning to another portion of the stand we found com- plete outfts for Tennis, Cricket, Croquet and other out- door Bloom, all by the well known firm of Smiley and Co., London. The part of the stage was devoted en- tirely to furnishing ironmongery of every description comprising table and floor lamps, an endless variety of brass and copper goods suitable for prosents, Cutlery, Coal Vases, Jardinieres in wrought iron, brass and cop- per, curb suites in all brass, black and brass, and black and copper. A novelty in the way of cooking utinsils was the" Royal Roaster." This is principally for roast- ing meat, poultry, So., and consists of an ordinary roasting tin, with a cover on top, which shuts the joint up, as in a bax. It prevents what is being coked from getting burnt and dried up and is guaranteed to make tough meat tender, besides keeping all the goodness in the joint and impar.:- ing a better flavour to the meat. Mr. Garland's stand also extended to the stage where a large plate glass case, containing every description of silver related goods was shown; also mail carts, carpet sweepers, &a. Taking the stands as a whole the firm are to be congratulated on tne excellence of their exhibit. We should mention that the sign was one of the finest in the exhibition, be- ing composed of solid polished brass letters mounted on a dark blue ground, the whole surrounded by a gilt frame.

Messrs. W. H. & C. LAWLESS. 5, Market Place, 47 Bridge-street, and Mersey Mount Steam Works. The chief exhibit and speciality of this firm in the corridor facing the entrance to the Hall is, the manufacturing before the public of all kinds of toffees, rocks, sweets and candies, Every day from 2 o'clock to 9.30 the pro cess of boiling is continued. The five specialities of the firm are :—Exhibition Almond roffee.Echibition Cream Toffee, Exhibition Chocolate Toffee, Eshibition Cocoa Nut and Lemon Toffee, and Butter and Treacle Toffee. Over two thousand tons of Toffee of forty varieties are on sale at twopence and sixpence each. Messrs. Lawless are also showing Jams, Marmalade, Sauces and Pickles, all of which are made at their Works. Mersey Mount. In the corridor there are over 100 varieties of biscuits, including the well-known "Ice Cream Wafer." We understand that the sale of this biscuit last year was so great that samples had to be withdrawn. In the centre of the hall is being shown a large sack of Chocolates : in fact Messrs. Lawless have over 150 varieties from ld up to 5% a bex. At one portion of this stand Cadbury's Cocoa Essence is being breve], and the beverage can be tasted and sampled. The firm of W. H. and C. We understand that the manufacturing of the Jams and Marmalade receive the personal attention of Miss Lawless. whilst Mr. Law- less gives the whole of his time to the other depart- ments.

MESSRS. J. H. PARKINSON AND SON, Of Bride-street' have a stand which should be of inters at to all ctciists There are cycle accessories of every description, and some important improvements are shown. Among them is the June( gear consisting of free wheel and brake. BY the" free wheel" is meant a back svh,a-A which will run freely, independently of the pedals. With pre " Juhel gear" fitted, the rider can let the machine run freely downhill with the pedals stationary, so that they can be utilised as footrests. At the same time the slightest backward pressure on one of the pedals brings the brake into action. The latter can be applied gradually, so that accordi g to the amount of back pressure tbe machine can be retarded 0 any desired extent, and if necessary brought to a standstill. Another interesting exhibit is the bicycle chain brush, the invention of Dar. William Parkinson. The contrivance clips near to the bottom of the tube in such a position that the bristles just touch the chain as it revolves on the gear wheel, and scours it of mud and dust. It is exceedingly simple and effective and should prove a boon to all cyclists who are without gear cases. The price is one shilling, alai already 53020 thousands are in use.

THE LONGFORD WIRE, IRON AND STEEL COMPANY, LIMITED, have a very attractive display. A sample of the last named is exhibited at- tached to a bedstead 6Ft. by 3ft.. carrying the stupen- dous weight of one ton, without injury to the wire met- tress or the bedstead, which demonstrates the superior quality of the firm's mattress and seat wire. Ma. ARTHUR W. BA.SE2, of the Excelsior gang' works, Winwick-road, Warrington, has a splendid case of wire gauges on view on the platf irrn of the hall. Mr. Baker has made a speciality of this line of business, and the gangee exhibited are those in use in the wire trade All his gauges from the largest to the smallest are trued up, and made correct both in size and the regular taper of the bell-mouthed nicks, after they are hardened, a fact manufacturers, drawers, and user; of wire will all appreciate, as any alteration that may have taken place in the hardening of the gauges is cor- rected b2fere going into the user's hands. He is, there- fore, able to supply quantities of gauges, in which all the nicks of the same denomination are exactly alike. Users well know the value of this, especially those who, having parchaeed a packet of gauges, whoie numbers represent there to be all alike, find that no two of them are so. His work in this direction shows him to have been most careful and painstaking in the finish of his goods. His exhibit contains imperial standard wire gauges, made in circular, double circular oblong, mei folding gauges. He has also special folding wire gauzes for rnantsfacturers' use, the nicks of which are made in alternative decimals, or one five-hundredth part of an inch between. These folding gauges, made light as pocket gauges, easily indicate no less than one- thouse.ndth part of an inch,and are excellent substitutes for and in many respects a handier toel than, the ordinary micrometer. His music wire gauges are well designed, beautifully made, and correct to sin. The same may be said of his pinion wire gauges, the marking of which is very convenient:for comparison, ac they are marked with the imperial standard numbers and their decimal equivalents in addition to the pinion wire num- bers, the decimals of which are on the back of the gauge, The whole exhibit is extremely well finished and reflects the highest credit on the maker.

BURNING ACCIDENT AT WARRINGTON. On Tuesday morning,snortly after eleven o'clock, Annie Buckley, ten years of age, residing with her parents at 1, Back nrossley-street, was taken to the Infirmary suff .ring from burns received through her clothes having caught fire at her home. Through the promptitude of a neighbour the flames were extinguished before any serious injuries had been infijcted. Atter treatment by Dr. Langdale the girl was allowed to be taken home. FIRE AT PRESTON BROOK.

Shortly afer one o'clock on Monday afternoon a fire broke out on the premises of Mr. Daniel Bee- nett, Red House Farm, Preston Brook. Under the direction of Superintendent Turner, a complement of men with one of the steam fire enginesproceeded to the scene and found four stacks of farm produce in a Dutch barn in full blaze. The stacks and berm were completely destroyed, the brigade just reach-, ing the fire in time to save the outhullainzs. The fire was discovered by one of the farm labourers. The damage is estimated at £3OO and is insured. The origin of the fire is not known. LDISIND CONIPOUND" (Trtde Mark) gives Ex- pectoration witnnit strain. 91d., 13s. Sold by Chemists only. —Advt.

THE sad death of Mr. Thomas The Death of Mr. Ellis. Ems, Chief Whip of the Liberal party, which took place on Wednesday,creates a gap inlLiberal organi. sation most difficult at this juncture to fill. Mr. Mass was in close touch with all the developments which Liberalism has undergone since Mr. GLAD. STONE disappeared from political life. He be-, longed to the new order of things inevitably asso- ciated with that event. Succeeding 111r,Mait.roiti- BANKS, on his becoming Lord TWEEDIIIOI7TH, Mr. ELLIS entered upon a sphere of duty with which he was not unfamiliar, but which at the same time. was all the more difficult to fulfil owing to the brilliancy of his predecessor and the chaos ensuing after Mr. GLADSTONE had cast the mantle of office upon Lord ROSEBERY.It may be confessed now that in those days the Liberal party was a hard flock to manage. There was discontent in some quarters with the leader. There was veiled jealously in other quarters against the painstak- ing young Welshman whose promotion in the counsels of the party had come so rapidly. In ad-. dition there was a whole new set of political pro- blems to be faced and,if possible,to be vanquished. In the strife of life men judge men by their works end very often little charity enters into that judg- ment. Mr. Ellis did not need charity. His work spoke for itself. No politician worked harder, or under more vexatious and trying conditions, and there is unhappily but too much reason for be- lieving that during :the trials of those .lays were sown the seeds of his early death. Yet before he died Mr. &Lis achieved a popularity in all parts of the House of Commons which is vouchsafed only to few men. His recipe was a simple one—tact- fulness, cosmopolitan sympathy, and that kind of genius which CARLYLE described as an, 'infinite capacity for taking pains. The late. WHIP took pains with everything he laid his hand to, whether it was an important Parliamen- tary division, a political dinner, or a recreative cycling tour, and his reward was political Success and personal popularity, the former not always complete, but "such success as men may come at by great pains." As we have already said, Mr. ELLis will be sadly missed by the Liberal party in the House of Commons. We are passing through a period of transition; the ship is sailing amidst a breeziness in the party ocean which would not call for uneasiness were there not signs that the breeziness may give place to a dangerous gale. At such moments the loss of one cf the chief mates is a serious matter for the most seaworthy vessel. Happily, in the Liberal ship there are several able seamen who are personally qualified for elevation even though they have not yat secured the techni- cal hall-mark of official approval. These matters can, however, rest awhile. For the moment the entire thoughts of Liberal members will turn with sympathy to the bereaved widow, and will sorrow-, fully reflect upon the great loss which she and chey have mutually sustained.

THE modest programme of the Twenty-five Independent Labour party is sure Seats.

to be commented upon with good humour. For a party with such exalted notions and such a leader as Mr. KIER HARDIE. and such a record in contests already fought, to be content with twenty-five contests at the next Genera! Election will be regarded as an evidence of temper- ate counsels and shrewd observation. We might have expected a much more extensive scheme, dad signed to striketerror into the kearts of all parties. We are thankful to have escaped so lightly, in the mercy of Mr. Hirdie and his doughty followers. They have been sufficiently prudent to abstain from by-election contests, because some previous affairs of this kind were costly, and they are con' centrating their strength and their resources upoli

===== 0002601_18990407_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
liventy-five seats, not yet located. Some more merciful members wished to defer the numerical resolve until the election was somewhat nearer, but they were beaten by nine votes, and so it is on record that by a majority of nine the party is com- mitted to twenty-five seats. Perhaps, if anyone had asked whether there was any chance of returning twenty-five members, he would have been treated aS a man of little faith, and promptly voted down. still, the question will be asked, along with some Others. If there is no prospect of returning twenty-five members for twenty-five seats to be contested, and the fun of the contests is the main thing to be desired, the csntraction of the game to twenty-five places seems i ather questionable policy. It might just as well have been one hundred and twenty-five. There would have been more money to find,but there would have been more fun for the expenditure. The men who want " sport" for their money usually distribute their bet3,and they find it delightful to watch the progress of events. The Independent Labour party is quite a gaming body. It delights in sport, in running candidates Who have no more chance than have some of the sPider-legged creatures upon whom lovers of great odds often put their money, in expectation of the miraculous. The joy of betting must very much resemble the fun of putting faith in candidates who never get a look-in. The province of sport in this --country is always growing; and now it has passed into politics we can but look on and wonder. It IS highly probable that of the twenty-fire candi- dates run for the twenty-five, as yet, unselected ~eats, not one will be returned. Apparently this Ls a small matter. There is flue sport in running them. Perhaps, on the whole, it is better that workingmen should put money on candidates than put it on horses. Personally, we regard it as a much higher form of sport, because a human be- ing is higher than a horse. Under these circum- stances, so restricted a racing card as twenty-five events seems but a poor show for a General Elec- tion. It almost looks as if there were some de- cline in the racing instincts of our advanced poli- ticians. Be that as it may, some good fun may be got out of twenty-five contests, and we may look forward to them with exhilarated feelings.

The • Sunday newspaper appears likely to materialise very shortly. Two big London dailies have expressed their intention of including the Sabbath among their publishing days. One is taking this step as an enterprise with the pre- Burnable aim of making an additional profit, and .the other is following suit solely in rivalry. This Innovation is a national affair, and not one Merely concerning the proprietors of the two Papers. It affects the whole populace of the country, and it threatens the sanctity of the Sabbath and the sacredness of the seventh day's rest. We must say at once that we emphatically disapprove of the idea. In the first place there is no need for the Sunday daily newspaper. ilusiness men in the quietude of their homes do not wish to have the world's worldliness thrust upon them on Sunday. It confronts them six days in the week, and is by no means desired on the SPenth. What the other half of the world is doing does not so immediately concern this half that it needs know all about it before Monday morning. Of Course there are now, and have been for years, newspapers published on Sunday, but these appeal o.nly to a certain section of the public and are of little importance compared with the possibilities and probabilities depending on the action of the London dailies referred to. There is every reason to believe that once the Sunday newspaper is started it will become an established fact all over the country. Competition will render this inevitable, and when it takes place the English Sunday will be of comparatively little use or value as a day of rest. Sunday labour will be entailed upon thousands of people—journalists, printers, com- positors, publishers, newsagents, newsboys, and many others who are affected by the ramifications and Influence of the great "fourth estate." As a Christian people we try to "keep the Sabbath lioly," but it will be practically impossible for us to obey this divine behest if newspapers are to flood the cities, towns, and villages every Lord's Day. The proposal will have to be generally dis- c.ountenanced and strenuously resisted if the Eng- lish Sunday is to maintain its unique character as a day of rest.

IVIr. H. W. Wilson is one of the sanest of our naval experts, otherwise his paper in the " Nine- teenth Century" this month could be passed over Without attention. Mr. Wilson is not satisfied With the present position of the navy. The serious weakness is the number of partially armoured, zuuzzle-loadin,c, vessels we possess. In another Year this weakness will become a grave danger, for ranee months ago undertook the rearmament of uer middle-aged ironclads, and when they are TeadY towards the end of next year they will in- creage the strength of her fleet by almost one- ftfl. It is therefore high time the Admiralty Pursued a like policy in England—taking in hand tWO or three ships at a time, working upon them, if necessary, night and day. Another vital point is that if nothing happens at the Peace Conference We shall have to provide for more new ships next Year. Otherwise our superiority at this moment will vanish, and in two or three years we shall be inferior. In the face of it we do not consider it Probable that the Admiralty has chosen this year for chanc,ino. their policy in regard to subventions to mercant cruisers. What has happened is that flr• Goschen is terminating, the existing contract -le order to obtain fresh concessions from the sub- aiclieed lines in the way of making the vessels •eafer in war time and speedier. The Americans are preparing to subsidise more merchantmen as a teault of the lessons taught by the recent war. It .14Poilld, indeed, be remarkable if Mr. Goschen in- 'llgurated a policy diametrically opposed to the experience gained last year.

The inter-works competition excites considerable in- terest. The other metches are between Wiggs v Spinch Yari; Soapery v Docks; and Castner Kellner v Hazleharsts. The weather so far has not been conducive to record gates.

ACCIDENT TO A CHILD AT RIINCIRN.

On Wednesday a sad accident occurrad to a daughter of Mr. William Brimelow, Church-street. Ic appears that the little girl who is about 13 years of age was playing in the yard, and attempted to reach some- thing from behind a salting-slate which was temporarily reared against the wall. The slate was o 7er-balanced and fell upon the little girl, causing a fracture. Dr F McDougall was soon in attenaance. but the poor little sufferer has sustained so much shock that at present little cin be done to allay her sufferings. Much sympathy is expressed with Mr. and Mrs. Brimelow at the sad occurrene3. MERSEY MISSION TO SEAMEN.
