PRESENTLTION TO MR. CLASH.

Oa Friday evening, after the service at the Runcorn Parish Cnurch, the members of the choir met in the ves- try for the purpose of making a presentation to Mr. Clare, who recently removed to Alderley Edge. On be- half of the choir boys Mr. Johnson (organist) asked Mr. Clare's acceptance of an inkstand, while from the adult members of the choir he presented him with a Bible bound io morrocco. Mr. Johnson referred to Mr. Clare's valuable services in the choir, and expressed regret that he had left the town. He had taken a great interest in the boys, and they all appreciated his kindness. The Rev. H. B. Firth and Mr. Branch also bore testimony to Mr. Caves praiseworthy efforts in the choir, and wished him success in- his new sphere of labour. Mr. Clare suitably acknowledged the gifts. The arrangements for the presentation were in the hands of Mr. W. E. Dudley.

Soss OF TEMPERANCE, .RIINCORN. An interesting meeting of the above order was held on Saturday evening in the Greenway-road School. There was a good attendance of officers and members. Mr. Thomas Cooke who occupied the chair, said they had a pleasing duty to perform, namely, to present Mr. Edward W. Key, with a beautiful emblem of the order, which real as follows :—" Order of the Sons of Temper- ance—Lifeboat Crew Division. Resolved, that the best best thanks of the subordinate division be and are hereby tendered to Bro. Edward W. Key for his valuable services as Worthy Patriarch for three terms.— THOMAS COOKE, worthy patriarch; JAMES JOHNSW, financial secretary." Tneir friend had begun when young to work in the temperance cause. For years he had been a cadet of temperance, and he was transferred to the adult order, in which he had filled a number of important offices with credit. He (the chairman) hoped his example would be a stimulus to the young men who were present. Ele was now the secretary of the cadets, and was following in the footsteps of his highly resp3c- ted father. (applause.) The following officers also spoke in terms of appreciation of Mr. Key's services : Messrs Daniel Bisbrown, Thomas Whyte, Thomas J 11 )lt, Thomas Clarke, James Johnson, and John Jones. Mr. Key heartily thanked them for their gift, and said that he should prize it very much.

THE COMITY OF RUNCORN'S WATER SUPPLY. The Water Committee reported that an analysis of a sample of water taken from the reeervoir had been re- ceived from Mr. J. Carter Bell, the county analyst, who remarked :—" As regards organic matter this is an ex- ceedingly good water but it contains too much chlorides •in the shape of chlorides and eolium." The follewing shows the percentage of free ammonia and chlorides per galloa :—Free ammonia 'OOlO5, chlorine in chlorides 391; Mr. J. Pritchard, as chairman of the Water Com- mittee, in moving the adoption of the report, stated that numerous tests had been taken during the year. They indicated that the water had undergone hardly any change. The water was remarkably free from any organic matter, but with reference to chlorides or eats, tbat remained about the same. Lest year showed a slight diaaunitiou, being Id grains per gallon, whilst the present analysis gave 39. Ac that twelve months was coming to a close they hoped to be able to lay before the next Council meeting the statement of receipts and expeediture for the year. , Mr. F. Wiswell seconded and considered that the analyst's report was a very satisfactory one. Mr. E. Liaison asked whether he could take it, from the fact there was a difference of two grains in the analysis, that the fissure theory consequent on the Ship Canal blasting operations at Weston Point, was ex- ploded, and whether being that the Council received quarterly reports and analyses upon the water it was usual to publish them ? Mr. Pritchard explained that the reason the analysis was published was because it was nearing the end of the year. During the year analyses had been taken but no material difference had been shown, therefore it was not considered there was need to publish them. Had there been a difference the committee would have at once put them before the public. Mr. Liaison further questioned regarding the fissure theory, stated that it did not seem clear to him that they should only have the one analysis submitted— which might have been the most favourable—for the benefit of the public. He considered that all or none should be published. Mr. liendleyrems.rked that the most satisfactory thing about the water was its freedom from organic matter. As the result of what he might call the agitation of a few years age they had had several analyses taken and there had been no organic matter shown in those analyses. They know that the water was hard,and would be hard, and as: far as any water in Cheshire was concerned they would find in it the products of Cheshire —salt. Mr. F. Wiswall for his own opinion, would go so far as to state that the diminution in chlurides exploded the fissure theory. For if there were any fissure tnere would certainly have been indications of organic matter show- ing itself, and certainly an increase of chloride. The mieutes were subsequently adopted.

Warrington Academy entered upon another phase ef its existence on Wednesday evening when it was opened under the auspices of the Warrington Society—an institution founded for the encourage- ment of local literary and artistic instinct. It will be remembered that but a short time ago the War- rington Academy,which is so rich in local memories, was in imminent danger of being razed to the ground. This irreparable loss was avoided by the public spirited action of a number of local gentle- men, and the formation of the Warrington Society may be considered to be the sequel to the agitation for the preservation of the building. Satisfactory terms have been arranged with the Corporation for renting the building, and it has now been sumptuously furnished and fitted up in a manner best suited to meet the wants of the members of the society and still retain a little of the atmosphere which surrounded the building in its academic days. The inaugural re- ception on Wednesday by the president (Lir. William Long, J.P.) was in every way a success. Am ,ngst those present were the Rev. E C E Carleton, R39'. John and Mrs Yonge. Rev. W H Drum-111nd, Alderman Henry Roberts, Councillor Arthur Bennett, Mr F Monks and Mrs Monks, Councillor Monks, Dr Gornall,and Dr Guest Gornall, Mr an Mrs R Garnett, J P., Mr and Mrs Fair- hurst, Mr and Mrs H S Page, Mr H J Westbrook, Mr Edwin Bradshaw, Mr and Mrs Crompton, Mr and Mrs Ewart, Mr and Mrs Hall, Mr and Mrs Kent, Mr and Mrs E Osborne, Mr and Mrs A Rs,thbene, Mr and Mrs W W Diaarnelow, Mr and Mrs Herbert Woods, Mr and Mrs Thomas May, Messrs. J A Kendrick, W Dilworth, Dunn, D3.W5313, Dawson, junior, and Grundy. The walls ot the rosms were decorated with a number of old War- rington pictures and portraits, and in the meeting room a collection of old Warrington literature was on view. A programme of music was provided by Mr. Percy Randall' band, and a number of pieces played were by Warrington composers. These included the marca "Mendelssohn" by A R Sutton; dance "Rtddon Court" by Percy Raadall; gavotte "Glen Mays" by R W Man- ning, and the waltz "The Blueloacks",by Walter °romp- ton.

Inaugural addressee were delivered in the meeting room during an interval in the programme. Mr WILLIAM LONG presided, aed in the course of a few remarks said it was a very pleasant commencement of his presidential duties to be able to welcome them into the old building of the Wet' inetoa Acedeiny. (Hear, he tr.) He was exceedingly sorry that the evening was so unp-opitious for them, and also very sorry that it so happened that those who would have been present were prevented owing to it being the holiday week. To show them that they hal a gaol deal of sympathy from those who were not present, he would ask Mr. Hall, one of the hon. secretaries, to read letters from these from whom he had received apologies. Mr. Ham, read the following letter from Dr. Drum- mond, remarking that it was a happy coincidence that at the present time they had his son living in the town : "Manchester College, Oxford. I am mueee obliged to you for your courtesy in inviting me te the interesting cere- mony in which the old home of the Warringtoa Academy is once mere to be dedicated to science, litera- ture and art. I as are you it would be a great pleasure to me to be present on such an occasion; and I must be content with a written expreseien of my gocd wishes for the success of the enterprise and of my sincere hope that the ancient building, with its interesting traditions will once m ere be a centre of intellectual ani moral culture in your town, and that members of all pertiee will meet together there with mutual respect and be drawn closer to one another, through mutual understanding, in CMIII/lon work for the public good.—James Drummond." Mr. H all proceeded to state that he had received letters of apeloey from the Mayor (Alderman Wright), Mr. L. Greening, Mr. and Mrs. V. B. Devies,Rev E. J. and Mrs. Willcoeks, Rev. Merley aed Mrs. Stevenson. Rev. Canon Willis, Mr. and Mrs. NI. Edelsten, Mr. W. D. Houghton, and Mr. Henry Greenall. The CHAIR:NUN after paying a tribute to Councillor Bennett's services. said they could not be too thankful that such a relic: should he saved to the town when they remembered the pasition which that building took as the home of literature and educational work at a time when such institutions were vary rare in the land ; the broa I spirit cultivated there at that time ; the tone it gave to the society of Warrington and district, enabl- ing it to be called—and he did not think it an empty boast—the Athens of the north. It was necessary in these days of close commercial competition when people were thinking chieey of their material wel- fare, of reoney and money making, that they should have their thoughts directed to the other side of life. He hoped the society which lied been formed would have a humanising ioflaence that would help to raise the tone of the place as he was sure the Academy had done in its earlier days. (Hear, hear.) If they succeeded in making it a home for the young of Warrington he should consider himself well repaid. (Hear, heard Councillor BENNETT said they had several objects in view in starting out upon the enterprise which had that night been brought to such a triumphant conclusion. Tneir first object was to preserve the building. They saw that nearly all the ancient buildings of the town had disappeared, including Cromwell's lodgings, Eyres' press the homes of Percival and B arnee,of Harrison and Littler- land:the shop in which Mongomery baked his bread and wrote his femme aPeeory ; arid also the new Academy. A few of the tutors' houses still remained but most of these seemed doomed to very shortly disappear. They felt the Academy was an ideal haildiee,atel if preserved would represent the most glorious epoch in the history of Warrington. (Hear, hear.) They were told that the building was old ; that it would detract from the street; and if altered it :could not retain any mlthetic ap- pearance. He ventured to think that what had been done with the assistance of the Borough Surveyor had greatly improved its appear- ance, and instead of it being an eyesore as some anticipated, it had become an ornament to the town. (Hear, hear.) The second idea they had in their minds was to definitely associate the building with memories of the old town's past, an I especially with the brightest period in their local history when Warrineton became " the Athens of Lancashire," if not of" England." They felt that the ideal solution would be to get the Museum Committee to take it over and use it as a reference library. The Museum Committee was somewhat re- stricted in its resources, and was not able te carry out the project. They therefore had to fall back on some other idea, and one feature was to make it in some sense the home of Warrington literature which was produced in such great abundance dur- ing last century. Another object was to have in tint building together with the old literature, the pictures of the men and women who had been the means of lending such a lasting lustre to the Academy. Their names were almost logion and it was almost impossible to believe what a laree number of brilliant men were assembled in Warrington in its academic days. The names of Dr. Priestley, Dr. Taylor, and Dr. Aiein were associated with that houee,and also Mac Aikin, afterwards Mrs. Berbeuld, who was prob- ably the greatest poetress of the last century. They would clod °a inspecting the rooms that they had already the nucleus of a library of old Warrington literature, and on the walls they bad the pictures of many War- rington worthies, more especially those associated with the Academy. Old Warringten worthies would therefore adorn its walls and old Warrington literature would line its ancient shelves. They had already made a good be- ginning. and further contributions would be thankfully received. Tee institution was not intended to be a rival ti but a fellow worker with the Museum. Recently he visited Manchester College, Orford, and he must say be was greatly struck with the esteem in which this old institution at Warrington was held by the respensible heads of the college. If Oxford was so proud of its connection with Warrington and was willing to proclaim this c man:7,lcm with the old town he thought they were not quite doing their duty unless they held these ancient rooms in honoured memory and male them a memento of that happy and distinguished time. It was not a sectarian or political institution and it was only in so far as it stood for art, literature, science and culture that they associated themselves with it. But whilst they wished to make the Old Academy at once a monument and a tIIkISOUCTI that was not enough. They wished to make it a home of some institution which was full of latter day life and energy, carrying out the traditions of the past in the present and the future. The Academy had there- fore been made the headquarters of the War- rington Society which nand for its object the encouragement of every form of local literary and artistic instinct. The society had been formed for the preservation of the Old Academy and of other ancient buildings and local monuments ; the col- lection of books, manuscripts, autographs, pictures,por- traits, silhouettes, engravings and other objects asso- ciated with the Academy or with the general history of the town ; the maintenance of the intellectual and ar- tistic traditions of the town and, neighbourhood ; the encouragement of everything which is calculated to im- prove the appearance and enhance the repu- tation of the town; the pursuit and enjoyment of science, literature, and art ; the establishment of subsidiary societies, clubs or other erganisetions to carry on these objects. In regard to the membership and the qualifications, it was well they should be made plain. There was no social qualification, and it was entirely unpolitical and non-sectarian. All who were interested in any of the subjects named would be wel- come to the extent of the accemmodazion of the rooms. The_v heel no objectien to billiards, but these were not their raison detre. They believed that people with intellectual tastes were just as much entitled to a decent dinner or a cup of unimpeachable coffee as other folk —(laughter)—and they would endeavour to provide it, but they did not wish anyone to join them for these reasons, but rather those chiefly interested in the " feat of reason and the flow of soul." They wished to make the Old Academy a congenial meeting place for the men of ant'quarian. literary, scientific or artistic tastes. Ladies were just as intellectual as men, and ever so

much nicer. (Laughter.) They were not in any way excluded by the rules, but the accommodation br the prelent was insufficient. They would, however, be allowed t 3 visit the Academy in company with members. Visitors would be allowed to see through the building, on 1 application to the caretaker. Housed in these congenial surroundings, with the faces of the immo7tals beaming on them from these jewelled walls, and their immortal words enshrined within their library they might hope to keep alive the best traditions of the town's past.- (dear, hear.) In an age of mammonism the Academy would stand for the eternal things, and amidst the smoke and hum of their extended commerce vrould point them to the blue skies and lofty thoughts agaid. (Applause).

SUNDA.Y EDITIONS OF DULY PAPERS.

EVPHATIC PROTEST &T RUNCORN.

At S. Paul's Wesleyan- C-h-u-re'--1,-Runcorn, on Sunday marning last, the Rev. C. W. L. Christian brought before the congregation the subjact of the proposed publication of a Sunday edition of the "Daily Telograph"and "Daily Mail." After an address on the great evils that would fellow if the proposal were carried out, he submitted the following resolution for their adoption, and after twice readinz it requested all in favour of it to riss,whereupon the whole congreg,,ation--q, very large one in spite of the holidays—rose in token of their hearty concurrence. _ _

" ft&solved :—That -t—his church and congregation de- sires to put on record its solemu protest a,gainst the proposed publtation of Sunday editions of daily news- pap'rs. It bolds (1) Tnat such publication would be an absoheely needless addition to daily labour, depriving multitude of that Sabbath rest so essential to the wea -being of mankind.

(2) That if once commenced it would naturally and inevitably lead to the general publication of Suniay elitione of papers that are not at preient contemplating such a step.

(3) That the introduction of such papers, with their ordinary contents, into the families of the people would be a widespread calamity for both old and young, bring- ing a flood of worldly thought and feeling into the day that should be freed from it and devoted to higher pur- poses.

(4) Above all it prate.ts against the proposal as a direct sin against Him, who, in infinite care for our physical, mental, and spiritual good has placed the Cam- mandrzent t " Keep holy the Sabbath Day "ia his moral law, which is for ever binding upon univerial man.

(5) Aud. while still hopeful that this proposal will be aba.ndotod, tne members of this church and congrega- tion pledge themselves never to purchase or advertise in any daily paper, which thus sins against their country and their God.

WARRINGTON AND SE CONDARY EDUCATION.

I SERIOUS POSITION.

Information has just reached us of a most im- portant character upon the above subject. For some time past it appears that the Association of Muni- cipal Corporations has had the subject under its most serious consideration and various attempts at a settlement have loam made. In January last the sub-eommittee me; and adopted the following scheme :-Ist "That a central education authority should be established, with an education minister attached" ; 2nd ‘` That a local education authoritf ; should be established in each educational area, for the purpose of deeling with secen iary education including technical instruction"; 3rd "That the area of every administrative county and ceun "At borough should be the educational area. A proviso was however introduced that, although the administra- tive county—which term includes for teie purpose a county borough—was to be accepted as the area for secondary educatiou, provision should be made enabling the council o.! any county or any berouge, to mike a representation to the effect that it was desirable that an educational area other than that of the ad ninistrative county should be constituted and the claims of such borough would be considered by the central autho- rity. This was held to mean that if any non- county borough was desirous of be3oming an edu- cational area it might apply t the ceatral authority. This eep to the non-county boroughs may be valued at any price but the conduct of the County Council of Lancashire towateis the non-county boroughs in the matter ot Technical Instruction precludes the possibility of assuming that any assistance to a non-county berough seeking to help I itself would be rendered ; rather is it to be feared that every obstacle would be placed in its way. The local education authority is to be appoin- ted as follows :—a majority by the County Council or coanty borough—if there are echosl boards one third of the remainder of the board— another third to be nominated by the education minister from the school boards and the remaining third to be co-optated by those already chosen. Where there are no school boards then the appoint- ment is to be as follows—a majority by the coencil of the county or borough ; one third of the re- mainder by the minister and the remainder cc- optated. This scheme, it will be at once seen, entirely shuts out all municipalities which are not county boroughs; and where there are no school boards in such municipalities, it will be further seen that such boroughs will have no voice what- ever in the selection of the education authority. It is impossible to conceive a greater hardship, be- I cause there are many boroughs having a 'ovulation larger than some county boroughs, yet, because the latter happen to be coanty boroughs, they are to enjoy a privilege that their more populous non- county neighbours may not have. The particulars of this scheme were forwarded to the Government and to the heads of the Education Department as the voice and desire of the municipal authorities of the kingdom and it was perceived that it must have considerable weight in the discussion by the Government of this somewhat difficult problem. When the scheme came on for consideration, not unnaturally, the non-county boroughs strenuously objected to what they considered as a deprivation of their natural rights, and, from what we learn, it , would seem that the question after being fully de- bated, and ably debated too, at a meeting of the Non-County Boroughs Aesociation,a resolution was unanimously passed, that the non-county boroughs should stand out for equal treatment with the county boroughs. So strongly and acutely was this , I unfair treatment felt that at one time it seemed I ' likely to be the rock upon which the very existence ; of the Association of Municipal Corporations might H split, and had it not been for the kindly feeling and strong desire entertained by the leaders of the non- county boroughs a cenflict would have been inevit- able. The representatives from Liverpool, at the meeting, where the matter came up for considera- tion, declared in the most arrogant way that, whether the non-county boroughs liked it or not, they meant to go on with the scheme as suggested. All parties agree that secondary education is an absolute necessity of our time,and that the carrying on of the educational plans can only be satisfactorily worked by a local authority. What particular form that local authority should be, cannot be settled by any off-hand resolution. It has been suggested that all boroughs with over ; 20,0)0 inhabitants should be the limit, but this I would not suit same authorities who are now doing good work in Technical Instruction. It has also , been suggested that the smaller boroughs should be I grouped with the villages around them, and thus ' centres of fair size could be created,' and very economically worked. It is no secret that the Tech- nical Instruction Committee of the County of Lan- cashire has been continually at loggerheads with I the local authorities and that in the opinion of many local authorities its conciuct and manage- ment still leaves much room for improve- ment. The publication of the proceedings of the Liverpool City Council shows that their representatives at the annual meeting of the Asso- ciation of Municipal Corporations did but state the truth when they declared their intention of acting at once, for the whole matter came before their Council on Wednesday night._

Now, let US see how Warrington will he affected by the propositions set forth. Warrington, as most know, is the largest non-county borough, and although we are credibly informed that the representatives of War- rington spoke out strenuously against the proposal—if this scheme be adopted Warrington will be effectually shut out. Many times have our columns proved the existence of friction between the County Council and the Borough, and threats that Warrington should seek to become a County Borough have been muttered time end time again, but now it is evident that some speedy action must I take place. Warrington has no School Board (which ' to many is a most unfortunate circtinistance), and therefore Warrington would have no representative at all upon the loc il educational authority, unless it should happen that the central educational au- thority acting under the proviso should be of opinion that it is in the best interest of education that a special area should be constituted for Warrington. The tenaper of the County Council, and the dispo§itien of its members, leaves no hope for the suggestion that they would welcome the creation of a special area for Warrington. If nothing is to be done in other ways therefore, Warrington must help itself, and the only effectual way of helping itself is for Warrington to take immediate steps to become a county borough. We have made enquiry, and have been informed that the great reason why Warrington has no: Fought to step up among the greater boroughs is that of expense— indeed there seems to be a certain fearful looking for, and dread of, any increased expenditure. It may, after all, be only a nightmare, and when the position of Warrington shall be properly worked up, and the indebtedness of the county to the town fully made out, it seems to us there is not much reason to anticipate any great increase of expenditure. War- riagton has now the reputation of being among the " forward movement" boroughs, and it certainly has shown no disposition to shirk its responsibility in dealing with technical instruction. The only complaint it has is the complaint of bad treatment at the hands of the County Council. It has secured a plot of land for a Technical School ; it has put ' into the new Bill a sum of money for the erection of such a school; it is carrying on success- fully continuation school-educational classes, art work, and all that pertains to technical instruction, and a grant from the rates has for some time been made, and we are informed that this item is again appearing in the estimates for the forthcoming year—all of which conclusively proves that War- aiington is not one whit behind in this matter. If, therefore, Warrington, is to maintain its position— anxious as it apparently is to do its duty—it must do more than talk or it will most probably find it- self merged in the larger authority of the county.

LATCHFORD PRIMITIVE METHODISTS. The Latchford Primitive Methodists held their congregational tea party the other day, when abcut 60 sat down to tea in the schoolroom, linuts- ford-road. After tea an excellent rendering of a beautiful flower service, composed by the Rev. J. Bailey. of Hampstead, London, was given in a manner, which created a marked impression on the bearers. The Rev. C. Pinch, senior circuit minister, presided, and Mr. J. 31as4ey played the organ ac- companiments. The service, which is based on the language of flowers, from a religious stand- point, and consisted of recitations. and sing- ing, was conducted by the following, and the rendering left nothing to be desired : —Misses Mary Cross (wild palms and mixed flowers . ). ===== 0002601_18990407_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
AFTER THE CONVENTION.

WHAT lIAS BEEN DOI`.TE,

===== 0002601_18990407_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
DY W. T. STEAD.

The National Convention and the huge miming,* ting meeting at the Queen's Hall are over, and are already but a memory—though a memory which most of those who were present will retain to the end of their lives. Never before has there been held a gathering similar to that in St. Martin's Town Hall, where congregated the delegates from two hundred town's meetings held throughout England, Scotland, and Wales. They were no more than some seven hundred strong—far fewer than at the initiation of this Crusade we had hoped they would be.

"To carve thy fullest thought, what though Time was not granted.? Thy great design shall stand." The words of Lowell come so aptly and pointedly that they might have been written purposely for the encouragement of the workers of the Crusade, who have spent themselves without thought of their. own convenience, and who at the end see unaccom- plished the aims with which they set forth on their three months of ceaseless toil.

Pessimism has temporarily seized some of those who have striven hard during many weeks, and now see so little tangible restilt. Yet neither pessimism nor disappointment was the dominant note of the Convention, but rather a feeling of splendid hope- fulness.. Perhaps that was, in some measure, due to the fact that the larger proportion of those present were from the provinces, where from the beginning the appreciation of what the Czar's Re.script may accomplish has been keener than in the metropolis, and where the enthusiasm for peace is far more real and earnest, and goes far deeper. From the opening to its close the Convention went with a swing. There was rather an embarrass- ment of riches in the way of speakers, and had it not been for the splendid tact and genial courtesy of the Earl of Aherdeen the Convention might have been rrolonged to the evening. As it was, the Convention lasted exactly for the two horits and ten minutes which was fixed beforehand as its natural limit, and in that time the whole of the resolutions were enthusiastically agreed to. After leaving the arrangements for the presenta- tion of the national and international memorials in the hands of the General Committee of the Crusade, the most important resolution come to by the Con- vention was that proposed by Canon Rawnsley, in a speech poetical and fervid, as are all his public utterances. The motion was not on the programme as drafted. Here are its terms: "That the local committees formed in various parts of the country in connection with this Crusade be urged to keep their organisation in existence, and that before the International Crusade of Peace is finally dissolved a special meeting of the General Committee should be convened to consider Mr. Stead's proposal for an extended and permanent organisation." That motion is the basis for a fresh start. When acted upon, it will ensure that no tittle of the effort which has been made during three strenuous months will be wasted.

In whatever the General Committee may decide to do for the future, there must be no interference with the existing peace and arbitration societies, which have from the beginning thrown themselves into the task of forwarding the Czar's efforts with commendable zeal and self-denial. Through many dark years, when the horizon has been unstreaked by one ray of light heralding the coming dawn, earnest and devoted men have laboured on, hoping against hope that sooner or later they will win the world to their views. They have been the evangelists of the Peace movement. By indomitable perseverance, they have smoothed the way over which the nations must eventually travel. It would be a thousand pities if anything which was now done should ignore the workers in the past, os should deprive them of one iota of the credit to which they are entitled.

.01uch may be achieved without trespassing on the ground already covered. God knows there is sufficient of the barren, arid, and sun-dried desert to cultivate without our venturing into the few patches of green which dot the landscape. We must strive to gradually enlarge the area of the tilled ground. For that purpose, we have in our hands the strings of a rough-and-ready organisation. In almost every town where a meeting has been hcld a committee has been formed. That com- znittee, in each case, contains earnest believers in the new gospel of the Czar. About this nucleus may grow up a strong society, doing all in its power to spread the doctrine of international friendliness and the brotherhood of man. In this way, the existing organisations may be reinforced and aided without being interfered with. The General Committee has other work before it. The National Convention left to the members of that body the arrangements for the presentation of the two memorials. Even with that task out of the vvey, much remains to be done. The International Conference at The Hague does not assemble until. May 18th. Between then and now, there must be the gathering of the harvest. We have, so far, accomplished little but the sowing of the seed. It remains to garner the abundant crop springing from the corn which fell upon fertile ground. A. small deputation gathering at The Hague during the sittings of the Peace Congress might be able to accomplish much by judicious advice wisely tendered. The possibility of sending such a. delegation of Peace experts will have to be con- sidered by the committee. Obviously, with so much work lying ready to its hand, the General Committee cannot think of dis- solving at the present time. The o,rganisation of the opinion of this country may be ' complete, but much more than this is wanted. The committee will not disperse until after the Powers have assembled through their representatives and come to some decision, good or evil, upon the Czar's pro- posals. Before the final separation, the accounts of the Crusade will be carefully audited by profes- sional accountants, and every man or woman who has contributed so much as one penny to the Crusade will receive a full account of the expendi- ture of the whole of the money. The amount is not so great as it should have been. Probably never before has so much been accomplished with resources so inadequate. Most of the newspapers spoke of the fine demon- stration at the Queen's Hall as the culminating gathering of the Crusade. I have myself adopted the phrase in this letter. Nevertheless the Crusade is not at an end. Several meetings have been held this week. Others are arranged for. These, how- ever, have not been organised by the General Committee. They are running independently of the central organisation, and are in themselves an evidence of the depth to which the feeling in favour of Peace has penetrated, apart altogether from any exertions made by the Crusade itself. With the present week the organ of the Crusade comes to an end. War Against War has been an absolutely unique publication. Other movements have had their news-sheets, but in no previous instance has a fully organised weekly paper been started for so short a period. The contributors have been brilliant men in literature and art, and the whole series of twelve numbers forms a unique and complete record of what will be an historic effort.

Now that the time has come to drop my pen it is well to ask, What has the Crusade accomplished All that we dreamt has not been realised. Even on the Continent, however, we have demonstrated that in every country where there is a publio opinion at all voices raised in protest against the huge burden of militarism rouse a wide responsive echo. In England we have proved that wherever men and women can be got together to discuss the great issues which lie at the bottom of the Czar's Itescript they speak with one voice in behalf of a Peace between nations and a drawing closer of the bonds which unite all peoples, whatever their differences of tongue, of colour, or of blood. It may be that the movement thus inaugurated will have its set-backs and its halts. At times its pulse may almost cease to beat; but sooner or later its principles will be the chief determining Cause in the policy of nations, • -

MR. d. F. SPANNER.

It is easy for us to explain the important truth revealed in the letter to which we invite your special attention. The important point of this letter is the superiority of Scott's Emulsicn over plain cod-liver oil. We fled this oil in Scott's Emulsion broken up and made into a form requiring almost no digestive effort, it is a great thing to make cod-liver oil palatable and easy for digestion, as is proved by the following letter : "13, AU Saints' Street, Landpart, Poitsmouta, September 24th, 1898.

Dear Sirs,—lt is with deep gratitude that I write thanking you for your valuable preparation, Scott's Emulsion. I can truly say it has been remarkable in my case. Being advised by one of the most eminent physicians of the town to try it, I did to, and I am thankful to say it was with most bene- ficial results. I rapidly improved in strength and vigour. I have been a great sufferer from bronchi- tis and emphysema. I tried cod-liver oil but my system was too weak and I could not keep it down, but as soon as I began your valuable preparation, from the first I felt its beneficial results, and where the plain oil failed, Scott's Emulsion succeeded.

Again thankieg you,

"I remain, dear sirs, yours sincerely,

" (oiened), J. F. SieTNNER."" We find in the feregoing letter an experience which is quite a familiar one and which cannot be repeated too often. 8o many people seem to think it necessary to burden their digestive organs with plain cod-liver oil, and consequently (hay ara abso- lutely unable to derive the benefits of its wonderful mr dicinal properties. In Seett's Emulsion you find cod-liver oil in a form ready for quick absorption. The oil in Scott's Emulsion is combined with glycerine and hypophosphitee, arid its potency is in- creased many times. Scott's Emulsion is extremely beneficial in all affections of throat and lungs, and wherever there is any indication of wasting of flesh and strength. It is pr!seribed by physicians for bronchitis, consumption, coughs,and colds,antemia, scrofula, rickets, marasmus,and wasting diseases of children. It restores vitality and strength without taxing the system.

You can obtain a sample of Scott's Emulsion by sending threepence to cover postage to Scott and Bowne, Limited, Manufacturing Chemists, 95,Great Saffron Hill, London, E.C., and mentioning this paper. All chemists sell Scott's Emulsion.

Trios were also given by Miss Ada and Ikelasters John and Percy Grounds whilst capital recitations were given by Misses Winifred Jones, B. Louie, and Mr Georee Whitefield. A sh.rt dialogue was given by Misses Lucy Marsh and Jane Houghton. The interest of the meeting however was centred in a speaking com- petition, which had been arranged, under the direction of the London United Temperance Committee, who pre- sented to the successful speaker a solid silver medal of beautiful design. There were ten competitors, all over 14 years of age, seven boys and three girls. The subject on which they bad to speak was "Why lam an ab- stainer," and they were not to go. beyond ten minutes limit. It is pleasing to report that each of the youthful speakers acquitted themselves very creditably and ad- vanced sound and solid reasons why they were abstainers hut the adjudicators, who were the Rev. W. Potts, J. Miller and A. H. Dunn were unanimous in awarding the medal to Master Bradford ,Kenworthy, and the second place to Sarah Jane Worrell to. whom was presented a book on behalt of the Warrington and District Band of Hope Union. Such satisfaction was expressed with the addresses of the other speakers that Mr. E. L. Jones preseeted each with a userul silver medal in the shape of a sixpence. Master G. A. Whitefield was also pre- sented with a book for good service during the sessions. Votes of thanks heartily given brought a successful meeting and a successful session to a close.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

THE KINGDOM OF GOD.

The subject of the lecture on Sunday in the Christa- delphian Room by. Mr. Finch of Wigan, was, as adver- tised "The Gospel of the Kingdom of God as prea:hed by Christ and the Apostles." The lecturer stated, that the subject they bad for consideration was of vital moment,for it dealt with the life mission of Jesus Christ. They would find on referring to Mark 1 and 14, that Christ commenced his public life by" preaching the gospel of the Kingdom of God; and this preaching con- tinued until he left the earth, his last command before being received up into heaven was to his disciples,whotn he told to "preach the Gospel" Mark 16 and 15. This work was continued after the death of Christ in accord- ance with the command. In Acts 19 and 8 was one of many instances which might be quoted,where they were informed that Paul " spake boldly for the space of three months, disputing and persuading the things concern- ing the Kingdom of God." Christ and his disciples laid down their lives, and the Gospel was sent forth through a sea of blood. Belief of the Gospel according to the scriptures was the only thing required to obtain salvation. This comprehended an acceptance of what he did, and obedience to His commands, for " the wcrd that I have spoken" said Christ," the same shall judge him in the last day." John 12 and 48. The message which Christ was sent with was "Preach the Kingdom of God," Luke 4 and 43, or" the Gospel "for this was the good news which Christ came to proclaim. This gospel was the power af God to salvation to every one that believeth, but they must discriminate hetween the gospel preached by Christ and the Apostles, and another gospel which was preached to day which had reference to the death and resurrection of Christ, an event which the disciples who were :Teachers of the gospel, did not andel stand Mark 9 and 32 and Luke 18 and 34 &c. People to-day made a pro- fession of belief in the Messenger, "Christ" but they did dot believe the message which God sent him to deliver. Seek ye first the Kingdom of God, were the words of Christ. and it was as necessary to do that now, as when the words were uttered, for a belief of the gospel was an absolute necessity if they were to have a future life. If the gospel be hid it is hid to them that Are lost,were the words of Paul and the lot of those who were lost would he to remain for ever in the congrega- tion of the dead. If they desired to have any part in t e good time coming when the Kinedom of God would be set up in the earth, it was absolutely necessary for them to render present obedience. It was useless for them to follow a way of man's devising, and he asked them to reject everything which was in opposition to the gospel for ‘the world by wisdom knew not Gorand "it pleased God by the fooli3hness of preaching to save them hat believe."

ST. PAUL'S CHURCH.

On Sunday the offertories at the above church were as arranged at the previous Easter vestry meeting, given o the Rev H V Pigot, who has accepted the living of Thomas' Church, Stockton Heath. There was a large --:ongregation at the morning service, when the preacher was the Rev H A Lister, who, at the close of his dis- courre, alluded to Mr. Pigot's forthcoming departure. In making an earnest appeal for a large collection he pointed out that the living of St. Paul's was not a large one ; many men working at the forges in Warrington had a better net income. The highly efficient state of St. Paul's Parochial Schools, the restored condition of the church, and a large and flourishing branch of the U.F.S. spoke of thenvelyee, and their offerings, therefore should be worthy of that unique occasion. The RbV H Gitson Smith. of Halewood, was the evening preacher. Toe offertories for the day amoonted to the handsome sum of £43 lOs 81.

STOCKTON HEATH.

HILL CLIFFE & STOCKTON HEATH UNITED BAND OF Here.—This flourishing society held its annual tea and entertainment, in closing the winter's work on Monday, in Providence Chapel, Stockton Heath. Tea was pro- e:ded in the schoolroom in the afternoon and about 150 members and friends enjoyed a good and ample spread the tables being • in cuarge of ladies from the three churches in connection wfth the Bard of Hope, viz: UU Cliffe Baptipt, Ebenezer Wesleyan, and Providence Independent Methodist. The public meeting and enttr- tainment was held in the chapel, which was filled to everflowing,there being many more at the meeting than at down to tea. The chair was occupied by the presid- ent of the society, Rev. J. S. flughes,who was supported by the Rev. W. Potts, Warrington,Messrs George White- field, A H Dunn, E Lloyd Jones See. There was an ex- cellent and lengthy programme. The Chairman offered a few words of welcome. The secretary, Mr. George Whitefield presented an encouraging report and appealed for lady helpers and the Rev W Potts gave an interesting and practical address. Sengs were given by Miss Emily Taylor and Mrs. John Fairhurst, whilst there was quite an orchestra of instrumentalists. Master George Arthur Whitefigld gave an auto-harp solo. Selections on the mandoline, violin, and piano were given by the Misses E. M., and: A. Winstanley from Lyrnm in superior style. Vieliriselos were also given by M•isters Arthur Dunn and Jo.:eph Houghton whilst Master Harry Davenport favoured the assembly with a clationet solo.

SHOCKING FATALftY ON THE SHIP

CANAL.

A COAL TRIMMER SUFFOCATED AT WALTON, On Tnursdav last week, at the Stag Inn, Walton, Mr. Woreley held an inquiry touching the death of George Webster, coal trimmer, who resided at Walton Inferior, and who was killed on Tuesday morning. Mr. John Whitfield, shipping azent, was present on behalf of Messrs. Richard Evans and Company, and Captain Waring represented the Ship Canal Company. Thomas Dunn, ot Moore, said he was a coal-trimmer at the Walton lay-bye. The steamer " Halstead " was coaling there on the 28th inst. The coal was, tipped into the ship with buckets with false bottoms. The coal fell into the boat in a wide form at the bottom and narrow at the top and the trimmers below had to level it. Witness rusd deceased were putting the air pipe on, standing on the coal at the top meanwhile, when the coal suddenly went down the hatchway. De.• ceased sipped with the coal and was buried under it. Witness also slipped but gripped a bit of rope attached to the ventilator and held himself up. He tried to save the deceased. Witness heard deceased call to the man below to get the c)al away. It was two hours before they got him out and he was dead. By Mr. Whitfield : There was room to have stood upon the after part of the ventlator where they would have been in safety. Peter Bodell, foreman of the men, said when he went down the hold, he saw one leg under bunker. He gave orders that the coal was to be moved from the shoot. The man was standing on the crown of the coal and he must wit have given a thought about the coal giving way so fast. Deceased was an experienced man. He believed he was smothered.

The jury returned a verdict that the deceased waS accieleutally emothercd by the fall of coal.

SANKEY BRIDGES WESLEYAN BOWLING CLUB. This Club commenced the season on April lat. The green, which is situated near Lovely-lane, was opened by Mr. Dauber, of Port Sunlight, who performed the c?remony in the usual way,and made some suitable remarks. The Committee are looking forward to a successful season. as the green is in excellent condition, the list of matches interesting, and the members enthusiastic.

THE RUNCOIVT GUARDIANS AND GRAND UNITED ORDER OF ODDFELLOW!i.

Sir,—Your report of the meeting of the Runcorn Guar- diana held on Tuincley loot is an exposure of the ignor- ance of several of the Guardians as to the purposes and objects of Friendly Societies. They are not termed to hand over their funds te the Poor Law Guardians. The rules of the Grand Unitei Order of Odlfellows etaten The objects of this society shall be to raise a fund or funds. , (a) For the relief of insmb.lrs during sicknees not caused by misconduct. (k) For the relief of members wheel compelled to travel in search of employment. (c) For insuring some of money for the purpose of defrayieg the futieral expenses of members, members' wives, widows acid children: These funds are provided by the contributions of the members who are entitled to benefits acc,arding to the amount o' contributions paid, no less and no more. The relief is to members not to P or Law Guardians. Within the limits of the Runcorn Guardians, the Runcorn and Halton and Rueeorn slistricts of tine society last year had 2181 members an 500 juvenile members. It disbursed to tne memb nn las ,ytr el .1k ply, £1,633 19. ; melical benefite, £283 es. 71., funeral benefits, £651 133 Od. diet-ess b esefi's, £3S 12s. 10d., or a total of £2,637 9s. 51. I ask the Guardians who uttered. cheap sneers at the name of 0 idfellows, whether it is not odd that working men should band themselves together, and be habits of thrift and prudence relieve the ratepayers in one year of the necessity for providing relief for so many who have bees is sickness and distress ? Were it not for Societies like this, many who keep oft the rates would have to apply for relief, and this applies to many of our memeese in this Union. Can they say to what eXteat this £2,637 relieved the Poor Rates last year ? The GA.7.0.0. ceneists in all of 266,185 members ; has a capital of £950,000 ; relieves its members yearly to the exteet of £1611,000, and has a record beyond the reproach of mete who are totally unacquainted with its objec's. As regards the lamentable case ref John Hazlehuret, the Rising Sun L dee in their letter do not decline to pee sick relief to himoelf bet they do decline to hand the f to the Gusrdianc—Mr. Reynolds' statement- that if the Lodge paid the money, Hazlehurst would not be a pauper is incorrect and before he talks things over again he should make sure of the correctness of his in formation. If the lodge did pay the 3s per week to the Guardians, Hazlehurat wou'd be none the less a pauper (mere's the pity) and as a matter of fact he has been a pauper some considerable tirne,heving been in the receipt of 3s pee week out-doer relief. no much for Mr Reynolds! Mr Stoke!, is either incorrect or incorrectly reported in stating that Hazlehurst did not know or was not aware he was going to the Workhouse. The facts are that Elazlehurst had lescome so feeble that he could not do anythieg for himself and his friends asked Mr. Cooke to remove him. Mr. Cooke arranged with Haziehuret a week previous to taking him to the workhouse that he should go there. 'foe Ledee had nothing to do with it. Ns one could be got to take charge of him as he was at 63 a week. The Lodge according to rules could only pay him 3s per week which was the amount provided by hi e contribu- tions and the Guardians I understand,do not relieve be- yond 31 per head. After Flaz'ehurstni entry into the workhouse had he had anyone dependent on him, his sick pay would be continued to them, as is the case in other instances at p:essnt, but the Guardians have no right to expect Rising Sun Lodl.s to pay them 33 per week. We relieve the rates indirectly but certainly do not intend to hand our funds over directly to the Guardians. We can make better use of them then they appear to te cap Inge of dente. Take the casts of the al- teration of the Board-room Sen, and the bungling con- nected therewith. Hezlehurst has paid his rates during his life, and let the Guardians bear the expense, which all our 2,181 members share as ratepayers. Why, should we be mulcted twice? The rule under which Rising Sun Lodge have anted has been in force several. yearn with the Manchester Unity and ether Lodge Friendly Societies. In fact it is conn- exion to them. Mr. Woonward may be a good judge of public scandals but I can assure him the Registrar- General would not have passed the rule had there been anything in it contrary to the good of the public and the members of the ssoiety and I suggest to him that h 3 de- velop as keen an interest in saving the ratepayers poc- lone when alterations and furnishings of .doarn rOoms occur in the future. Hazlehurst is still a member of ourso- ciety and We shall pay his funeral expenses to his nominee whoever that may be. The lcdee has no complaint from Bro Hazlehurst nor dispute with him. He joined at age 39 and has paid 21 per month which was the scale in force at his entry. Since then, new tables have been ifisued by the actuaties and a man aged 39 wishing to join would now have to pay 33 4d per month. Notwithstand- ing that Bro Hazlehurst has not paid his proportion of contributions he has always received full sick, half-pay, and deferted pay according to ruleer. He has I believe, no sickness, but is the victim of old age and although the society does not provide old age pleaione he has re- ceived an equivalent as deferred sick-pay, tu which according to the rules he is not entitled. The lodge has acted generously with him and is quite willing to con- tinue so to donif he is ever able to leave the house. I wish to express the thanks of the members to Mr. Meidowcroft, 'who appears to have been the only Guardian present who understood the rule—Yours tee., WILLIAM MASSEY, Secretary, Halton and Runcorn District G.U.0.0. Runcorn, 30th March, 1899.

SAILING RACES ON THE MERSEY.

On Saturday afternoon the Warrington and Bank Quay Sailing Club gave their second annual sailing race on the Meriey. EARLESTOWN & NEWTON.

THE CHURCH SOCIETY FOR WAIFS AND STRAYS.

A special sermon was preached in the Wargrave I Church, on Sunday morning,in aid of the Waifs and Strays by the Rev. J. Still. Podmore, B.A.,secretary to the Church Society for Waifs and Strays. The collection realised .C 2 is 2d. CONFIRMATION AT WINWICS. At the confirmation service held at Winwick Church on Wednesday last, at which the Lord Bishop of Liverpool ofticiated, there were 52 candi- dates presented from Wargrave, made up of 34 males and 18 females. P.S.A.

At the usual meeting-o-f-the P.S.A. Society held in the Co-operative Hall, Earlestown on Sunday after- noon, there was a fair attendance. Mr. Markland presided, and Mr. W. H. Dale was the principal speaker. A couple of sacred selections were sung by Miss Stonach who was in capital voice. ATTEMPTED SUICIDE AT EA.RLESTOWN,:f.

On 7Tuesday morning a man named George Frederick Archer, lodging in Wargrave-road Earl 64; town was removed to the Warrington Infirmary suffering from self-inflicted wounds to his throat. It appears he got upon Tuesday morning to go to his work, and on his nephew going downstairs an hour or so later, he found Archer lying on the kitchen floor with his throat cut. It was found on his being medically examined that the wounds were not of a very serious character, but he was re- moved tp the Infirmary as stated. THE EASTER HOLIDAYS AT EARLESTOWN..

--- _ The holidays at Earlestown and Newton have been of the most miserable description. Rain heralded the opening of Good Friday, and it con- tinued to descend until after noon. Many were the disappointments c weed, and the numerous cycling tours had to be abandoned. Fortunately many people had left the town on the Thursday for dis- tant places,and they thus escaped the deluge which would have proved most inconvenient. In the afternoon garlestown played an alliance fixture with Whiston, and with the inlucament of danc ing after the match to the strains of the Earles- town band, there was a large crowd present. Satur- day was a considerable improvement on the previ- ous day, and consequently a large number of people were induced to make a flying visit to Liverpool, Manchester and other places. In the afternoon Eeriest own -football club met Adlington at Haydock in a replayed semi-final tie for the Wigan Cup, and. this was sufficient attraction to take over 1,000 people from home. Mo•iday morning was again wet, but it cleared a little in the afternoon, but be- sides a football match at Haydock, there was no attraction in the town. The Viaduct works, and Messrs. Vicars recommenced operations on Tues. day morning, but the Vulcan Foundry did not re- open until Weduesday morning and Messrs. Slee and Co. on Thursday. :47,

BANE HOLIDAY AT WA.II,IIINGTON.

The weather ou Monday (Bank Holiday) wgs ex- tremely we; diving the morning, but it cleared up somewhat towards afternoon. Happily many people had left the town by early excursion trains to Black- pool and other places,and large bookings were taken at the Central Station. Tee numbers of excursion- its were ;—Liverpool and New Brighton, 2,750 : Manchester, 1,500 ; Belle Vue, 100; Southport, 500; and a considerable number of people booked to Matlock, Buxton, and Bakewell. The "A " team football match and the trades exhibition attracted many people during the afternoon otherwise the day would have been unusually dull.

THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, APRIL 7, 1899.

THE THREE 110IIRS SERVICE ON GOOD FRIDAY.

Mr. Foulkes asked if the Vicar had the sanction of the Bishop for the three hours agony service en Good Friday. _ _ .

Mr. Linaker said the Vicar was not bound to answer the question, and he should decline to do so. The Vicar said the three hours service was with the knowledge of the Bishop of Chester.

TRH LENTEN PREACHERS. Mr. Foulkes asked if the Vicar was aware that the Vicsr of Latchford (one of the Lenten preachers) observed the east ward position in his own ehurch. He produced a ritualistic book which, he said, stated that this was the position cf the sacrificing priest, and was adopted by members of the Elglish Church Union. Mr. Reynolds said Mr. Foulkes was out of order. _

Mr. Foulkes said there were three clergymen among the L,nten preachers who adopted the eastward position and if the Vicar did not know these men before he in- vited them to preach he could easily ascertain for the sum of is el. The clergymen he referred to were the Vicar of Alvanley, the Vicar of Lostock Gralarn,and the Vicar of Latchford. As a rule these men were false teachers. On Friday last he went to hear the Vicar of Latchford, and he told them something that was not true. Mr Foulkes moved a resolution to the effect that clergymen who were members of the English Church Union, or who adopted the eastward positioo,snoald not in future be invited to preach. The Vicar said he refused to put such a resolution to the meting. It was entirelyout of the authority of that meeting to decide such a question. He was not respon- sible to the parish for what he taught. He was respon- sible to his bishop. Mr. Foulkes : That's a qumtion, sir. THE VICAR AND MUDDLE-HEADED MANIACS.

EASTER VESTRY MEETINGS.

HOLY TRINITY CHURCH. RUNCORN.

The Easter vestry meeting at Holy Trinity Church, Runcorn, was held on Wednesday evening, under the presidency of the Rev C R Killick (vicar). There were also present :—Messrs C A Timmins, W Fisher, W Banks, S Pearson. E R Westbroeke, W J gunaingleara, G W Gibson, R lEllcross, A C Timmins, H Timmins, Sec. —The Vicar iced a letter from Mr C A Timmins (vicar's warden); "Dear sir,—Easter is rapidly approaching and I shall be glad to be relieved of the duties eVicar's warden when it comes. I have held that office for seven years and I think you will be able to find several in the parieh who would worthily fill the post and relieve me of one of the anxieties that has been such a strain upon me daring my spell of ill health, and which although much improved is not up to its former standard." the Vicar felt that Mr, Timmins had been called upon during the past year to undergo a great deal of domestic trouble which be was sure all the parishioners very much regretted, and they only hoped that his health and that of his family might be better in the feture. In accept ing the resigna:ion they must thank him for what he bad done for them. He (the Vicar) nominated as his warden Mr. William Fisher, who had very worthily filled the office before and whom he had not the slightest donbt would do all to the satisfaction of the parishioners and himself.—Mr. Fisher proposed Mr. Hankinson as people's warden. Mr Hankinson had served the people for several years, and he did not think they could get a better man for the office.—Mr.Westbrooke said Mr Han- kinson could not he present, but had expressed willingness to act if elected.—Mr. Killcross seconded.—Mr. Timmins said he had pleasure in sup- porting the proposition and thought they would have a combination in Mr. Fisher and Mr. Hankinson that they would be very well pleased with.—They were two of the eldest members of the church. (Hear, hear.)—Mr. Hankinson was unenimously elected. On the motion of Mr G W Gibson, seconded by Mr W J Cunningham, a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Mr. Timmins and Hankihson for their past services, and the following gentlemen were elected sidesmen :—Holy Trinity : Messrs R Killeross, S Peaeson, S Hazelburst, E Prince. E Westbrooke, W E Lear, J Savale, J H Salkell, T E Garner, B Turner, T H Annett, A Abram, W Bankee, —Halton, and C H Timmins, Mariner's Mission Mr J Hood; Mill Broar Mission: Mr W _

Cunningham.—Mr. 'C A Timmins presented the account for the year which sh4wed the total receipts for Trinity to be 2137 33 41; out of which amount £lO 19s 41 had !von voted to liquidate balance against the Mariner's Mission. There was a balance 3s Id remaining at Parr's Bank. In addition Mr. Timmins said that there had been several small bills receivel since amounting to £6 .6slld;but they would be cwered by receipts amounting to £7 123 2d, thus increasing the balance in hand by 21 5s 31. Mr. Timmins then went on to state that from 1881 to 1892 (12 years)tbe total collection and donations amounted to £1,005 3s. From 1893 to 1899 (7 years)the total collections and donations was £llB7 33 11d, which gave an average per year of £169 93 54, an amount

Which had only been exceeled four timog since the consecration of the church, viz : in 1850, 1856, 1869 and 1873. In conclusion M. Timming intirn tted that though he wag about to sever his official connection with the church they could always rely upon his sympathy and support. THE CHURCH COUNCIL CRITICISED.

Mr. Foulke; asked for the production of the resolution pased by the Frodsham Church Council with a view to Preventing the Rev. C H Wainwright,vicar of Blackpool, from coining to lecture in Frodsham. a copy of which was sent to Mr. Wainwrizlit and the Bishop of Manches- ter. He thought it was a downright shame that any number of men should propose such a resolution. Such ! men stisuld live by themselves in the island of St. Helena. It was a record worthy of the dark ages. He was plemed to know that all the members of the Church Council did not agree with that resolution, and he thought if the Bishop of Manchester's reply were pro- duced the people of Frodsham would know that he had snubbed the Council very niMy. Every man, be he clergyman or layman, had a perfect right to address a public audience in a public room. He did not Fay he had a right to c)mi, into the church without the sanction of the vicar, but he had a perfect right to go from one end of the country to the other to address public audiences. The lecture by the Rev. F. E. Powell in the schoolroom was ful of utter nonsense and rubbish, and the vicar and the senior curate did not (Ater to utter a word of protest. Mr. Colston, tho curate, only three or four days ago, could delight his audience by attacking John Kensit in the Iron Church. It was a pity that he had not something else to do than attack a man behind his back. John Kensit was able to defend himself. He moved that the Bishop of Manchester's resolution be read to the meet- ice'. The Chairman said the corresponiollos with the BIB- ' hop of Manchester belonged to the Church Council, and it did not fall within the province of that vestry to make any such demand as Mr. Foullcs had put forward. The Bishop of Manchester instead of sending a snub, as Mr. Foulkes put it, wrote a very kind and courteous letter expressing his regret that Mr. Wainwright should have given offence by his lecture, but it was out of his power to curtail his liberty. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Foulkes said Mr. Wainwright's lectures were crowded. .

Gray I handed over to Mr. Pigot sums of money connected with organizations in the pariah amounting to £33 193 su remarking that every fund had a good balance in hand. The penny bank was in a very nourishing condition, and the accounts had been audited by the public auditor. ihe books were still in his hands.- -- sir. Woods took advantage of that opportunity to say a few words of thanks to Mr. Gray, woo had been with them for several years. They had always worked most amicably together, and it was evideat from the large congregation which assembled in the church on the pre- vious evening that Mr. Gray possessed the good-will of the whole of the parish.—Mr. Richardson seemded, and said he could endorse all that Mr. Woods had just said with regard to Mr. Gray, who had been with them for three years. The services had been conducted in a very effieient manner. (dear, hear.)—Mr. Rose, as organist, and Mr. Davies also added their testimony to Mr. Gray's worth and work, the latter commenting upon the peace- ful manner in which the work of the parish had been conducted during the past twelve months. They wished ' him well in his future career, and hoped that when he came near the parish he would net forget his old friends. —Mr. A. Down also spoke in a similar strain and said that, as deputy organist, a pest he had occupied for 10 years, he could testity to the good fceling that existed between the choir and Mr. Gray.--After a few words from the Chairman the motion was put to the meeting and heartily agreed to.—The Rev. S. P. Gray, in reply, said he thanked are speakers for their kind expressions with regard to him, and he alsa took that opportunity, to thank the congregation for the Easter offerings given to him. One accepted that as a still further expression of goodwill on the part of the congregation. The :brae years he had spent in the parish had been happy years, and it was with a feeling of deep regret in many re- grets that he would leave his work at Stockton Heath. It was, at the same time, satisfactory to know that there was now a permanent representative of the church in the person of Mr. Pigot. (Hear, hear.)—Ttie meeting ooecluded with a vote of thanks to the Chairman.

TRAGIC SUICIDE NEAR WARRINGTON.

Mr. F. A. Jones, deputy coroner, held an inquest at the Eagle and Chili, Rixton, on Tuesday afternoon, OR the body of Elizabeth Ball, aged eighteen years,dauglater of a publican of Lougtoa, Staffordshire, who had fer the past three weeks been in service at the Eagle and Child, and who committed suicide by jumping into the Ship Canal on Sunday.—An uncle of the deceased identified the body, and said that on Saturday afternoen he came to visit his niece. Sae me:, him at the station, and ap- peared then to be in good spirits. Shortly after wards, however, she complained of being unwell, and went to bed. He did not again see her alive. She had left home on two occasions because of unpleasantness, but he knew of no reasen why she should commit suicide.— Mrs. S stnnah Alieney, wife of the tenant of the Eagle and Child. Rixton, said deceased had been in her employ for three weeks, and always appeared to like her place. She only appeared despondent when she spoke of home. She had mentioned that some timh age she had injured herself by falling from a chair, and she sometimes suffered great pain in consequence. —A labourer on the Ship Canal camel Marsham stated that he was going te his work at Rixt)n at ten minutee past seven on Sunday morning. When near Warburtora Bridge he heard a dog barking furiously. It kept runn- ing to and fro near the canal. He went to see weat was the matter and found a woman's hat and fur boa on the bank, and looking into the canal he was jut in time to see a woman sinking. He shouted to a passing steamer and a boat was put out. The canal was dragged, and four hours later the body was receverad.—The jury re- turaFd a v.irdict of " Suicide during temporary in- sanity," and expressed the opinion that the insanity was caused by the sufferings in consequence el the injarg she had received.

A WARRINGTON MAN CEIARGED WITH ILL-TREATING A PIG

At the Warrington Courvy Potty Sas donsson Wednes- day, before Messrs. Claude Morrie (in the ch lir), John Crosfield and Joshua Ball, Ricoar I Roberts (assis;.ant to Mr. Mynekyne, pork butcher, Market-street. Warring- ton),w as chArged with having on the TA March, at Walston, croeny ill-treated and abused a pig by kicking it. Mr. Browne appeared for the defeece ani denied the charge.—Ellen Fairhurst stated teat she had sold some pigs to Mr. Mynekyne. The defendant came to her house to remove then nto Warrington. Tney had t he taken from the pig sty to the float. The animalg "were awkward," and the defendant kicked them. One pig coald not walk and "then he started to kick it on the head." WitU,s3 was understood to say that the de- fendant's kick broke one of the animal's legs,lt was about eight months old.—By Mr. Browne: The pig had been lame a few days,but it had got all right.lt had slipped in the cote. The defendant could not get the float near the sty.—Mr.Brawne,for the defence,said the case had been brought into Court merely owing to a dispute, Mr and Mrs., Mynekyne having made a deduction for tha broken leg of the pig. Mrs. Fairhurst, who was a highly respect- able woman, admitted that the animal had been lame. The defendant was in the service of Mr. Mynekyne and he was sent to Mr. Fairhurst's for the pigs. He found when he arrived that he could not get the flaat near the cote, and that the animals were whet was called "frost- bitten In tee limbs." The result was that he had te get the assistance of a boy to almost carry the pigB from the cote to the float. He never kicked any of the animals. The injury complained of must have been done a fortnight or three weeks previously.— The defendant was called and denied the charge a cruelty. He did not kick any of the animals. Be simply used his leg to move the animal's head to get it along.— Charles Brown, pork butcher, in the employ of Mr. Mynekyne, said that when the animals came from Woolston he found that one of them had a broken leg. There won) external bruises, and the injury must have been inflicted a fortnight or three weeks.—The Chair- man told Me. Browne that he need proceed no further with the case, which would be dismissed.

LATEST NEWS.

A. WARNING TO BAZA.A.R. PROMOTERS. Yeterda.y an important decision was given by the Widnes magistrates, which should have an effect upon local baz tars where lott nies are often indulged in. John Lythgoe, confectioner, Mersey-road. was fined 20s. and .costs or a menth for keeping a lottery at his shop. People paid a penny for a dip into a basket containing packets of sweets, which held tickets entitling the winners to twopennywrth of sweers. The magis— trates described it as a pernicious system. FATALITY AT A ROPEWORKS. AN OXFORD sruDENr KILLED. At the ropeworks of Mei3r3. Htggie, Sunderland. Kenneth, youngest son of Mr. D. H. Haggie, J.P., was yesterday fund under an engine terrinly mutilatec., having been entangled in the machinery. Death must have been almest instantaneous. He was a promising young man, well-known in the no:th and at Oxford, where he was being educated. INDIAN OFFICERS BITTEN BY A MAD DOG. SIMLA, THURSDAY. Captain M. A. Tighe and Captain Charles Archer, political agents in South-Eastern Balucinstan and Zhol respectively, have been bitten by a mad dog, and have started for Paris to be treat Al at the Pasteur Institution. The fact that besiies private persons 33 British soldier have been sent to that establishment is regarded as em- phasising the strong need of an institution of this kind in India.
